## 2. 2026-03-03
<a id="t-2028839921272635452"></a>

*Single post · [2028839921272635452](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2028839921272635452)*

remember that the tape shapes the news and not vice versa

few thoughts:

1/5

past few months reminded me of this tweet below + the tape at that time (3/31/2025)

before "liberation day" the nasdaq traded like a bag of ass every single day for ~2 months...bleeding lower.

hence why ppl who have been trading mkts for a long time got that eerie feeling - "we could crash."

that's captured in the tweet below, the notion of, it's not about the catalyst. the catalyst could be anything.

2/5

markets don't crash from all-time highs. its when you have a "sick" tape with weak leadership, just sort of floundering -- like we've had recently -- where the mkt is vulnerable.

dont look for news to backfit px action - just respect px action. ppl don't do this -- hence "omg a 2028 substack bear case crashed the market"...my man, it definitely did not lol...do not insult the market's intelligence like that.

but that gets us where we are now...the headline "war in iran," 3-6 months ago, a strong tape shrugs that off + rotates $ into unaffected sectors. 

the impact of "liberation day" type events, is not discrete - it's shaped by the underlying health of the tape.

3-6 months ago was a rotation tape. now... based strictly on px action, when you see things like "the nikkei just sold off 7% in a straight line," you may be in a correction tape.

3/5

no need to try and shoehorn news around it. the mkt tends to sniff out peaks in EPS growth, it tops out and turns lower.

doesn't mean the cycle ends - could sell off, base for 6 months, and let stocks get cheaper while waiting for new leadership to emerge + liquidity/stimulus from all angles + handoff from secular to cyclical growth.

but don't sit here expecting that we're down on a headline and will go up on the next headline. read the tape...its unhealthy, don't ask why, don't search for reasons why, just respect it.

4/5

"stop doing what isn't working."

watch the 52w highs list - 203 names with $2B+ in mkt cap printed yday...it's just not the 203 that everyone owns. 

you can buy software -50% ytd or buy petrobras +50% ytd. what twtr is doing isn't important - look at the tape and you'll see what "they" are doing. basic mats/energy/industrials are roaring.

the indices could get bashed around a bit, but real downturns never happen with cyclical growth is going apeshit to the upside.

5/5

so -- "DON'T READ NEWS, READ TAPE." 

there is no news, there never is, news will just put you on the wrong side of the trade. 

internals + movement under the hood, are your best guide as to what happens next...

mind ur risk + good luck trading.

## 4. 2026-03-04
<a id="t-2029021620900139054"></a>

*Single post · [2029021620900139054](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2029021620900139054)*

few quotes from the big dog on dealing with hardship & adversity --

Munger: “Assume life will be really tough, and then ask if you can handle it. If the answer is yes, you’ve won.”

Munger: “Like Nietzsche once said: ‘The man had a lame leg and he’s proud of it.’ If you have a defect you try to increase, you’re on your way to the shallows. Envy, huge self-pity, extreme ideology, intense loyalty to a particular identity – you’ve just taken your brain and started to pound on it with a hammer."

Munger: “Life will have terrible blows in it, horrible blows, unfair blows. And some people recover and others don’t. And there I think the attitude of Epictetus is the best. He said that every missed chance in life was an opportunity to behave well, every missed chance in life was an opportunity to learn something, and that your duty was not to be submerged in self-pity, but to utilize the terrible blow in constructive fashion."

> QT @gregoryblotnick: Munger Refresh: Independent Thinking, Disdain & The Power of Incentives

## 6. 2026-03-04
<a id="t-2029174566157734369"></a>

*Single post · [2029174566157734369](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2029174566157734369)*

things that are supposed to "work" in investing/trading but actually have a 0% success rate

-sotp

-buybacks

-"paying a dividend will create/expand into a new yield-oriented shareholder base"

-"how did IR sound" "how did the CFO sound" etc

-RSI

-mean-reversion of any valuation metric or multiple

-debt to GDP, entitlement spending, other doomer pitches that end with "and then the 30-year goes to 12%"

-"wait until the market realizes that the depreciable life of their office kitchen microwave is 5 years and not 6"

-NAV discount

-M&A synergies

-ERP implementations (unless ur short)

-if 2nd best player with 30% margins can get to 1st's 40% margins...catch up trade --> 50% upside. then you learn its structural

-any/all intraday trading color esp on progress of any "big client" order - if an elephant doesnt want to leave a trail, there's infinite ways to do so thru swaps/derivatives/etc. same for all "unusual options flow," if they dont want to be seen, u aint gonna see em

-"an activist could get involved here"

-"email the ceo and tell him and other insiders to buy some stock"

-"the AI overhang will lift as long as the quarter is okay"

-short squeezes

-lock-up expiration

-analyst day trade / conference day trade / investor day

-"I could see PE getting interested here around 5x EBITDA"

-variant view on FX for the qtr

-shorting Deere

## 7. 2026-03-04
<a id="t-2029229068965523713"></a>

*Thread · 2 posts · conversation `2029229068965523713`*

### 1/2

*2026-03-04 · [2029229068965523713](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2029229068965523713)*

someone asked, "what DOES work?"

there's only one answer:

REVENUE UPSIDE TO THE QUARTER.

acceleration -> stock goes up
deceleration -> stock goes down

100% certainty doesnt exist in markets. this is the closest you can get. 

[MS/BCG]: "The #1 Driver of TSR = Revenue Growth."

-M&A synergies

-ERP implementations (unless ur short)

-if 2nd best player with 30% margins can get to 1st's 40% margins...catch up trade --> 50% upside. then you learn its structural

-any/all intraday trading color esp on progress of any "big client" order - if an elephant doesnt want to leave a trail, there's infinite ways to do so thru swaps/derivatives/etc. same for all "unusual options flow," if they dont want to be seen, u aint gonna see em

-"an activist could get involved here"

-"email the ceo and tell him and other insiders to buy some stock"

-"the AI overhang will lift as long as the quarter is okay"

-short squeezes

-lock-up expiration

-analyst day trade / conference day trade / investor day

-"I could see PE getting interested here around 5x EBITDA"

-variant view on FX for the qtr

-shorting Deere

### 2/2

*2026-03-04 · [2029234732291199180](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2029234732291199180)*

fwiw -- that chart is illuminating if you've ever tried to short accelerating revenue growth/SSS%.

biz can be structurally unprofitable...unit economics don't work at any level of revenue

mkt wont bite.

this is super important to understand esp if you're new to L/S and short-selling in general

you will never get paid on EBITDA/EPS while revs are beating and raising, save for rare "bought the comp" / GM% implosions

this sounds like a simple lesson but I have probably lost more money shorting dogshit businesses with accelerating revenue growth than anything else.

because it wasn't clear to me that this rule is damn near ironclad. if a stock is "revenue today, profits manana," your model for that stock should have one line item

revenue

thats it. throw the rest of the fuckin model in the trash...the real wise man waits for them to miss #'s and then shorts it down 25% bc he knows its going down 80%.

most ppl shorting stocks dont grasp this, they jump in the way of momentum over and over. took me FOREVER to stop doing that shit - risk mgmt matters more than any individual short position.

be patient, if its as bad a business as u think it is, ur better off with a lower cost basis + lower risk, vs higher basis but fighting rev accel/momentum

example: $100 with upside mo vs. $80 with downside mo -- you want the $80 every time.

this doesn't become intuitive until you've been shorting stocks for many years and suffered immense brain damage.

also have heard it said that if its going to zero, whether u short it at $80 or $40 is irrelevant -- $ position size is the same and 100% max gain is the same.

if you subscribe to that point of view, you may as well wait for the 2nd miss as confirmation that the story is totally broken, then short it down 50%...some wisdom there, the 1st miss you still got the "true believers."

TL;DR - treat that rev growth as an ironclad rule and never assume u will get paid until it rolls.

---

## 10. 2026-03-09
<a id="t-2030993861518963184"></a>

*Single post · [2030993861518963184](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2030993861518963184)*

very important step for one's development as an investor or trader, that I wish I'd begun much sooner:

process vs outcome: documenting all events/catalysts by hit rate + taking a data-driven approach to everything you do.

(1/3)

biggest mistake ppl make, is not rubbing their nose in their own mistakes, or asking: "was I right for the wrong reason?"

I bring this up bc in the thread below, there's all sorts of binary setups listed...like "investor day" trades, or "lockup expiry" trades...all event-driven.

many of these are coin-flips, or 50-50 bets -- those are really your worst odds in markets. it's going up or it's going down.

but if you get a bunch of coin flips "right," and assume, wow, I have a natural knack for these, you're making an error in judgment.

(2/3)

La Rochefoucauld wrote that "self-love is more cunning than the most cunning man in the world" -- that's what you're trying to combat here. your mind will convince you that you're smart, when in reality, "good outcome" + "bad process" = WRONG.

so for something like a lockup expiry trade, if you've gotten say 10 out of 15 right, you have to ask: luck or skill? am I good at broadly analyzing these setups or are they stock-specific reads?

biggest Q always has to be = "what was my actual P&L?" meaning realized P&L... you have to evaluate the entire life-cycle of the trade...execution from start to finish. "I got the initial stock move right" doesn't equal "right," if on day 3 the trade realized red P&L.

(3/3)

this kind of brutal self-efficacy separates good from great... most ppl don't want to spend a Sunday afternoon staring at all their losses.

but without this ongoing self-evaluation, sorting everything by process vs. outcome, batting/slugging avg, you're not really making progress.

the path to determining what you do well, your "circle of competence," is mostly defined by years...if not decades... of steadily X'ing out all the things you DON'T do well.

> QT @gregoryblotnick: things that are supposed to "work" in investing/trading but actually have a 0% success rate

-sotp

-buybacks

-"paying a dividend will create/expand into a new yield-oriented shareholder base"

-"how did IR sound" "how did the CFO sound" etc

-RSI

-mean-reversion of any valuation metric or multiple

-debt to GDP, entitlement spending, other doomer pitches that end with "and then the 30-year goes to 12%"

-"wait until the market realizes that the depreciable life of their office kitchen microwave is 5 years and not 6"

-NAV discount

-M&A synergies

-ERP implementations (unless ur short)

-if 2nd best player with 30% margins can get to 1st's 40% margins...catch up trade --> 50% upside. then you learn its structural

-any/all intraday trading color esp on progress of any "big client" order - if an elephant doesnt want to leave a trail, there's infinite ways to do so thru swaps/derivatives/etc. same for all "unusual options flow," if they dont want to be seen, u aint gonna see em

-"an activist could get involved here"

-"email the ceo and tell him and other insiders to buy some stock"

-"the AI overhang will lift as long as the quarter is okay"

-short squeezes

-lock-up expiration

-analyst day trade / conference day trade / investor day

-"I could see PE getting interested here around 5x EBITDA"

-variant view on FX for the qtr

-shorting Deere

gave LLMs another go with AMZN over the weekend (grok/claude/gemini/chatgpt)

1. "give me last 10 years of amzn revenue" - close to perfect; chatgpt messed up 2024/2025

2. "give me last 10 years of amzn fcf"

output was problematic

amzn's "headline" / company-defined fcf, is lease-adjusted - this is usually the # at the top of press releases

they also give regular "FCF"

the highlighted green cells are where they at least tied to regular fcf

for 2025, claude/grok used (CFO - capex) as fcf; gemini got the right # provided in the PR by mgmt ($11.2).

other than that, claude and grok were the "best," chatgpt seems to consistently be the "worst," none are perfect or even seem to be consistent in where they pull #'s from

proceed w caution + always check vs company source docs

lays out in simple terms how markets operate:

## 18. 2026-03-25
<a id="t-2036764892384968925"></a>

*Single post · [2036764892384968925](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2036764892384968925)*

prob #1 instinct to develop for surviving markets is a “ding” sound, like when an airplane pilot says “you are now free to move about the cabin”

except the “ding” = you saying “I have no fuckin clue whats going on” --> cutting/exiting

"if you're going to panic, panic first" is an old truism for a reason...hard to explain until u have watched a really good trader/pm/risk mgr in action

good = cuts losses fast...diff bw good and great = speed at cutting losses + comfort acting on incomplete information + blind trust in gut instinct

bc you see them take a loss down 10% - "this makes no sense to me" - and then time passes, and by the time it makes sense to everyone, its down 30%

esp as an analyst, ur frustrated at first at getting liquidated w/o thesis "confirmation"

but they know...u were wrong down 10%, u get "confirmation" down 30%...and that 20% "gain," while invisible in $, is every bit as real -- and actually worth >20% when factoring in Mental Capital

you see this fast trigger finger over and over and thats how they stay in the game and never draw down. if none of this makes sense to u, someday it will, u havent lost enough $ yet

no different than in war, great commanders know...a good retreat can be just as valuable as a victory.

## 20. 2026-03-25
<a id="t-2036817824677511649"></a>

*Single post · [2036817824677511649](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2036817824677511649)*

really important advice I got at a younger age

"Be nice to the juniors -- because someday, they may be your boss."

many other reasons...i'll pick 4

1. after u have been fired 1000x, crashed out, been out of work for long periods of time, u will see that u NEVER know where that life raft is gonna come from.

ppl u expect will let u down + ppl u had counted out will step up.

but people junior to you, who you mentored, or opened a door for, or got them a job...are about as loyal and grateful as anyone you know.

and if u think of ur own loyalty and gratitude to people who mentored u, or opened a door, u will see why this is true.

2. most people are only nice to seniors/their boss/ppl they think can open a door for them. so you're already in the minority if u abide by this rule

3. the age gap rapidly shrinks as time goes on. 20 vs 25 seems like a lot...but 30 to 35 has very little difference...and 50 to 55, even less.

skill and talent don't take long to overwhelm any difference in years.

if someone is an absolute rockstar at a young age, they will have hit a level by age 30 that others dont hit until 40 or later

u just never know...kid at 22-23 who seemed alright, u blink, suddenly hes running $3B and knocking the cover off the ball

4. last, basic humility.

Fortune is a fickle mistress, men rise and fall, sometimes many times throughout their lives, and circumstances shift in ways no one could have never seen coming...have to stay open to the ways of the universe.

## 21. 2026-03-29
<a id="t-2038249383309828523"></a>

*Single post · [2038249383309828523](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2038249383309828523)*

"Familiarity Breeds Contempt."

very old proverb but one that u will find urself re-learning all across life

relationships, careers, friendship, love

it's an unpleasant concept but has to be internalized esp for work

(1/4)

photo below is from a bio on Roosevelt:

"Roosevelt accepted such compliments graciously, while being careful to avoid the familiarity which would assuredly breed contempt."

then goes on to explain this in practice

it's not just politics though, this applies for everything work-related -- has some ties to "is it better to be loved or feared."

this concept is unpleasant to me because it basically requires affectation thru any lens of work/politics

effective bosses or leaders u worked for, who seemed cold, or distant, or "asshole" to you... are just people who understood this hard truth of life:

"familiarity breeds contempt."

(2/4)

as u get older and have more and more ppl under you, or investors, or any blurring of the line between friendship and business, u have to be mindful of those three words.

also file under "Setting Boundaries."

I don't have the answer as to how this is best done, I struggle with it, I'm just bringing it to ur attention in hopes of helping u think differently.

at the most basic level though, this happens in many business relationships where u become friends too quickly -- and u recognize that they're afraid to give u direct feedback. mistake I've made a lot. u have to just bucket them as "friend," end the business side, find a replacement, and do a better job setting boundaries

(3/4)

in friendship this concept tends to be pretty straightforward

the things we find amusing at first, or that we put up with...end up being things we can't stand about our friends.

but friendship at its core is based on tolerating one another's faults - I'd argue that it's the only definition

a true friend will put up with your endless bullshit and set aside any contempt they have for your defects of character.

(4/4)

in love...this is where "familiarity breeds contempt" reigns supreme.

at the surface level, with someone u just met, too much familiarity too fast drives them away...it destroys whatever intrigue was there, shows that there's no mystery or depth to you...expressed as "he came on too strong," u moved too fast, u revealed desperation or that u arent a challenge.

once u successfully get past that phase...every long-term relationship ends up being defined at some point by the ways in which "familiarity breeds contempt."

the things u tolerated at first, u end up HATING about one another. everyone has lived thru this at least once.

if there's no communication, these little things quietly build and form resentment...which grows and grows until it all reveals itself in a blow-out fight

those can sometimes be devastating/point of no return

there is no solution to this, other than "communicate," because familiarity WILL breed contempt.

this is why the best advice is along the lines of: dont ever take someone conditionally, thinking "if I change x y and z they'll be perfect."

those won't change. u can't change people.

make sure that the flaws they come with, are ones u can live with...because they only become more glaring as time goes on.

END>>

this post could be a lot longer but I think the point is clear...its an unpleasant topic, an unfortunate truth of human nature, and one that if not internalized, will cause u problems in all facets of life.

"avoid the familiarity which assuredly breeds contempt."

---

## 22. 2026-03-29
<a id="t-2038289474229092810"></a>

*Single post · [2038289474229092810](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2038289474229092810)*

I think about this Flaubert quote a lot as far as discipline + ppl looking for "kicks" and variety in the wrong places

avoiding the monotonous grind, the dull grey horizon, that characterizes success in just about anything

1/5

perfect example is trading markets which is brutally boring when done right, and where if you're having fun, you're probably about to blow up

goes for fundamental L/S which is 99.9% updating models...or buffett doing nothing for years on end

most great traders are just getting stopped out +  taking small losses all day

you can tell people this, they'll nod, and then go buy a bunch of options expiring in 3 hours

2/5

same for resistance training - "do squats, lunges, pull ups, and pushups"

a week later they'll ask what else they should do...brotha that's it, I just told you lol

diet...eat this this and that

few weeks go by, "what else can I eat"

always looking for excitement in the wrong places

have to think about food as fuel and thats it. get ur thrills elsewhere...not stuff like diet or exercise

3/5

lost track how many DM convos i've had like this

"I want to be an equity analyst"

"okay, heres a template for a model and a stock pitch - build a bunch of these, send me one when you're done, I'll critique it and help u improve."

a week later, no model, no pitch - "what else should I do?"

thats it, hermano...nada mas.

u see this over and over and over. I can only imagine how many times Kobe told someone "go shoot 1000 free throws a day" followed by "okay but what else?"

I have empathy, but no sympathy

discipline is boring. it's the "shit test" for everything, the screening mechanism. if it was easy then everyone would be great

4/5

thats why this framework is useful

"regular + orderly" vs "violent + original"

all the stuff mentioned above...be regular and orderly

the important things tend to all fit this bucket

then, u find other outlets in ur life to be violent and original...write, speak, build, create, travel, pursue hobbies

5/5

I forget where I saw this but someone phrased it as, have a hobby where if you break concentration, you could die

which means that things like tennis or fishing or golf dont qualify

go rock climbing, skiing, skydiving, fighting, anything aerial, anything deep underwater

I've had several near-death experiences skiing at a billion mph but there's no denying that u enter a meditative state

so i'll leave this open-ended on purpose. most ppl prob end up chasing sex and drugs as a release for their violent urges...I have faith u can find a better option, one that's more sustainable at least.

choose violence responsibly™️

> QT @gregoryblotnick: "Be regular and orderly in your life, so that you may be violent and original in your work." Flaubert

## 23. 2026-03-29
<a id="t-2038355262441136153"></a>

*Single post · [2038355262441136153](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2038355262441136153)*

most important lesson I learned in my 30’s is that you cannot manage other people’s perceptions of u.

I vividly remember being 34-35 and REALLY struggling with this so I'll explain.

(1/7)

people don't think, they feel.

in your mind u assume that everything about u is being weighed rationally and using the power of reason

in reality, there is no objective reality.

we live in stories. 

we have stories of ourselves and who we believe ourselves to be. 

the other people in our midst are comprised of stories that we have subjectively written about them. and you, in turn, are nothing but a story to others. there isn't just one "story" - each story is subjective and unique based on the individual author in question.

(2/7)

to some people, u are viewed thru a favorable lens, a favorable story. everything u say or do is filtered thru this lens.

their view of u is skewed (and incorrect) in that it's too rosy. their perception is too positive.

to others, u are viewed thru a negative lens, a negative story. ur words and actions are painted with this brush on arrival.

their perception is skewed and incorrect, just as the other person's was -- the only difference is the directional bias. one is too positive, one is too negative. 

in both cases, this is confirmation bias at work, and neither individual may even be aware of this information-filtering mechanism as it takes place.

(3/7)

what you need to understand, is that you are powerless to change either of these two perceptions.

"you cannot manage someone else's perception of you."

put another way, what other people think is none of your business.

humans are wildly irrational. if you haven't noticed, people perceive all sorts of slights or insults that were either unintentional, and quite often, never even occured. imaginations run wild and ppl dream up scenarios where someone is their best friend or worst enemy - with zero basis in fact.

(4/7)

you will drive yourself CRAZY trying to empathize "why does this person think what they do?"

because the truth, most of the time, is that they don't even know.

the subconscious is something like 95% of brain activity on the high end, or "the vast majority of it" on the low end. we have a very, very limited understanding of what makes us think, feel, say, and do the things that we do.

at some point, your actions, which are straightforward and logical to you, will be perceived on a wide spectrum by others. this will baffle you - how can all these people draw different conclusions about what I say or do, or even who I am as a human being?

(5/7)

but this is reality.

you are perceived differently, you have a different "story," in the mind of every single person you will ever meet.

is there room for their perception to shift positively or negatively based on your actions - of course.

yet how much power do u have to control their overall perception of you? very, very little...a number, for your own sanity, best rounded in your own head to zero.

(6/7)

the output is "stop caring what other people think." this is not meant in a sociopathic or thoughtless light. it means that u should do what u believe to be right, and recognize that ur "sphere of influence" extends no further than that.

you are powerless to shape how your words or actions are interpreted by the mind of a given recipient.

there is intention, and there is perception.

you can control your intention. you cannot control their perception.

so don't overthink it, and as a broad way of operating, don't bother trying to manage how other people perceive u... because u simply can't.

in the words of Buffett: “I have this complicated procedure I go through every morning, which is to look in the mirror and decide what I’m going to do. And I feel at that point, everybody’s had their say.”

(7/7)

paradoxically, what u will find is that u become a better person once u stop caring what other people think. 

it is very easy to bend over backwards trying to please other people, or at least doing what we perceive shall please them.

in this process, it is very easy to do the wrong thing while believing it to be right; because it wasn't done for you, it was done for the sake of other people.

yet in trying to manage other people's perception of u, u will not only drive yourself crazy, seeing as it doesn't work, but u will likely go astray, go against your own gut instinct, and effectively betray yourself.

by short-circuiting all of this -- simply doing what you know in your heart to be right, giving it no further thought, and controlling only what lays in your control -- u will find yourself achieving better outcomes with less stress.

that's why the lesson is so important.

hth

## 25. 2026-03-30
<a id="t-2038653451643519451"></a>

*Single post · [2038653451643519451](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2038653451643519451)*

advice I wasn't given until my 30's, that someone REALLY should have given to me in my 20's:

"Always be interviewing." Always, always know your "fair value" in the market.

Take every intvw, get an offer in writing, then push for even higher comp and get that in writing.

Go back to your current firm and say "you're paying me X, I have an offer away for 2x but I'd rather stay with you."

They'll match the offer because if you leave, they have to search, hire, screen, train, etc...from a time and energy perspective, its always easier to just pay you more.

There is no loyalty in corporate America, especially in finance... this is something everyone learns the hard way, and the truth is that nothing is ever personal.

All you're asking is to be paid what you're worth. These types of negotiations are literally why HR depts exist.

Some of the guys I've known in L/S who made the most $, they're not the most talented.

They're expert negotiators who somehow mastered the politics of bouncing from firm to firm, pod to pod, securing guarantees and bonuses and getting progressively higher comp each time.

Others I know who are insanely talented mgrs or analysts, but are humble individuals... they're out of work.

Despite L/S being fairly meritocratic, it's no different than the rest of the world... "everything is sales," and pitching stocks < pitching yourself.

I was never very good at this, usually I was the dumbest guy in the room and skewed towards "be humble, loyal, quiet, and don't rock the boat." 

But that's wrong, I was wrong, it's not how the world works. Don't be like that.

There is a quip that I think comes from Schopenhauer -- merit and modesty have nothing in common except the initial letter.

That is how shit really works. Merit doesn't win out...if there's a guy with less merit, but who wants it more, or who can sell the fuck out of himself, he will win. 

I have no angle here - this is a skillset I wish I had cultivated at a young age. 

If you have merit, wonderful -- having both [merit + sales] will take you much farther than merit alone. Make yourself into a dual threat, there is literally no reason not to...know what you're worth, ask politely to be paid what you're worth, and if I had to guess, you will be amazed how often you get it.

That's the advice I wish I was given in my early 20's. Good luck

## 28. 2026-04-07
<a id="t-2041471614479483243"></a>

*Single post · [2041471614479483243](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2041471614479483243)*

infinite ROI habits / immunizing urself against the vicissitudes of fortune / self-discipline and "HABITS FOR LIFE"

(1/7) - go on youtube, watch a perfect push-up + pull-up + tricep dip + squat.  then film urself doing them on your phone. you will see almost immediately where u are fucking up

once u get those right do a total "reset" mentally...whatever u thought ur max was, delete it, no one cares anyway. then start fresh, high reps with perfect form, use 20 lb weights, use just the bar or 135, wherever you're at, then slowly move higher

I'm not a fitness account but I'd almost guarantee that in 3-6 months ur results will be better than before
 
2. asset-liability mismatch

not sure if there's a better term, but the more variability u have in ur career and life in general, the more important it is to be robotic on all this little shit

founders, entrepreneurs, traders, stuff like that...these are chaotic career paths...u want to NAIL DOWN all the little shit in ur life as a counterweight

I lost the source of this but most failures arent from lack of talent or a bad gameplan...its from burnout or bad lifestyle choices/habits, compounded over years.

3. eat the same thing every day

for as many meals as possible...ask the 3 people u know who are in the best shape, they'll tell u this is exactly what they do.

there's really five, breakfast, mid morning, lunch, mid afternoon, dinner. LLMs are prob ur best bet - there's like 20-30 "perfect foods" - figure out what those are and just eat those + random spices and sauces...treat it like business

4. when I say habits for life, that = next 50 years. life changes fast...nailing this little shit down is how u remain steady as a rock

ur current circumstances are not permanent. u want habits that u can keep in place for ANY set of circumstances. thats the point of all this

u might have a great life today, tomorrow u get fired or ur biz fails, then ur lady walks out and takes the kids with her. shit like this happens every day, don't be ignorant or naive, if it happens to others it can happen to u

if u can shrug and say "well, I know 80% of my day tomorrow is still gonna be the same" thats how u stay mentally and emotionally resilient. gonna wake up, train, eat the same shit, and then slog through hell

the Stoics figured this out 2000+ years ago, I'm not saying anything revolutionary here, but ppl are always looking for new answers where none exist...everything about human nature/habits/living a good life, the Greeks figured out.

5. go to bed and wake up at the same time every day. prob the most important thing that nobody does - bc its invisible in a sense

but it causes a lot of mental/emotional turbulence when u break it

life gets in the way a lot, sometimes ur out late or traveling, or up in the middle of the night for any # of reasons...everyone has the same shit going on...do ur best

have to at least try and put urself in bed, physically, 7+ hours before that AM alarm

when u study great men/women throughout history, the "in bed" time changes, but they hold that AM alarm sacred...get up at the same time, dont break that.  if ur lifestyle allows for a nap later, great, if not just suffer for a day and then go to bed earlier that night

6. "the power of good habit" is every bit as real as the power of bad habit

once u knock out 7-8 hours of sleep multiple nights in a row, u REALLY feel a 4-5 hour night

once u eat clean for a month, then eat some processed food...u dont even shit ur brains out, its the reverse, it just disappears lol. and u feel like garbage

if ur in the gym daily, then skip a day...u feel antsy all day, anxious almost

this is all ur body rebelling + "the power of good habit"

be robotic...when ppl u know are asked, "who is the most disciplined person u know," the answer should be ur name. bc unlike most of life, its 100% in ur control

study kobe, study brady - u will see that in championships and 2-min drills, they activate ROBOT MODE and destroy everything in their path. that stems from being robotic on daily habits

7. "cognitive load" - Zuck wearing a hoodie every day...this is one of those things u laugh at at first, but theres something to it

I think the idea is to avoid cognitive load in the AM deciding what to wear

and while it sounds silly, its not the hoodie - its the broad mindset, the attitude towards habits.

the less u think about this little shit, the more brain u have for what matters

if ur in a city, all black everything, black t's and polos, dark jeans, few pairs of gucci loafs or tods...in many cases, the expensive shit is the cheapest bc it lasts forever. if ur somewhere sunny like FL its even easier, pretty much all-compression everything

when u wake up, u have a full battery, which depletes throughout the day...so if u can get "wake up + go to the gym + eat breakfast full of protein + get dressed" on autopilot, those are habits for life that never change and will keep u young forever

END

its funny bc some of this shit, its so simple u feel stupid writing about it

but the truth is, a man becomes his habits, and perfecting the little things u do every day has by FAR the biggest impact on ur future than all the other dumbass shit and "lifehacks"

king solomon already told u...there is nothing new under the sun.

hth

## 31. 2026-04-16
<a id="t-2044706054454313271"></a>

*Single post · [2044706054454313271](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2044706054454313271)*

"I want to start posting and writing under my real name but I'm afraid what friends/family will think"

this is common and an age-old question

I'll pass along the best advice I've gotten or read on the subject, 3 things

first, you have to look back at every author in history and ask: "is this something they faced?"

the answer is obviously yes

its just a cost of doing business, price of entry, etc

this applies far less for business/self-help (bland, sterile) and more for the stuff worth writing about (life, pain, hardship) where u can really impact the readers life in a positive way.

2nd, on family...I've heard crazy stories (present-day) where a woman wrote a novel, like fantasy/fiction, totally innocuous, and her mother didn't speak to her for two years

I want to say "as long as you leave them out of it you should be okay" but its tricky bc if you think of some of the best books you've ever read...they took it there. "educated" by westover comes to mind.

the boilerplate advice seems to be "they'll get over it," which is true. family you're stuck with for better or worse, blood is blood.

3rd, on friends, I think this is the easiest part

if your friends don't support you putting yourself out there, get new friends.

sadly an old truism that "your friends want to do well...but not better than they're doing." 

La Rochefoucauld: "in our friends' misfortunes, we always find something not displeasing to us"

but you'll figure out real quick who is envious, toxic, negative, vs who is positive and supportive

the ones who mock you and try to chop you down...just smile and keep it moving.

as for the really good ones, if you lead by example, they'll follow you.

"a man's life comes in seasons" - no bad blood or ill will - as u start a new season, sometimes the cast of characters will change with it...que sera, sera.

put yourself out there. good luck 🫡

on using Twitter less / the eternal war against social media addiction

I've found it most helpful to have "Twitter days" and "non-Twitter days"

weekends, and then say Wednesday, are non-Twitter days

I know from experience that if I post something, it becomes a Twitter day

If I post something in the morning I somehow don't have the focus for reading books all afternoon/evening

I'm crazy grateful for the followers I have and genuinely enjoy engaging w/ people, idk how I got here but I feel like I'm bottom 5% percentile of intelligence...so many replies or DM's have me like, "how the FUCK are you so smart???"

nasty people and mean-spirited criticism is w/e, that scales linearly as u grow, u cant avoid them...check any big account...no amount of moral perfection can save u, they'll find something to denigrate.

the amount that u need to stare at ur feed "for work" is prob 1% true and 99% addiction/self-deceit/a negative esp for markets...think back to 2 weeks ago...guarantee u got sucked into consensus + groupthink and failed to make the correct play.

so... that leaves 4 "twitter days." the amount of ideas, tweets, articles etc that come to mind doesnt change - it actually grows on off-days (turn off ur phone for a day and watch ur creativity skyrocket).

this means you will end up posting a lot on those days. I ask myself: "is this a problem? the accounts I really like, do I care if they post a lot?"

honestly, no. this app is 99.9% garbage, slop, ragebait, engagement farming...I would say its the worst I've ever seen it and somehow continues to get worse, esp in times of war, where America's enemies know this is our weak point. why is Tucker Qatarlson Network in my feed pounding the table on Islam, really weird shit like that

so if you are a real human with something interesting to say, I have no problem with you flooding my feed, I'll read it all.

hopefully this helps. think back over the past 2-4 weeks of "monitoring the situation" and ask what it did for ya...NADA. zero or negative Return On Time.

read less tweets and more books

## 34. 2026-04-17
<a id="t-2045199923679809567"></a>

*Single post · [2045215891021779334](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2045215891021779334)*

forgot, on "its priced in" + the meme below

every decision can be bucketed as either Arrogance or Humility

Arrogance: "I'm right, the market is wrong."
Humility: "I'm wrong, the market is right."

it's an old Klarmanism that investing is really just a balancing act between those two

same goes for risk mgmt - if you like something at $100, and it goes to $50, do you see opportunity or risk?

no right answer...Buffett would say opportunity, someone like PTJ says risk. 

the idea of trading with stop-losses is non-negotiable in the latter camp, but is heretical in the former.

all just Arrogance vs. Humility.

my opinion isn't worth a shit but I'm "Default to Humility," I just have too many scars.

and so this “it’s priced in” meme, while satirical, actually represents the Humility camp -- and as silly as it sounds, it has prevented an ungodly amount of blown accounts.

as the old trader truism goes, there are two rules to the game. one: if you don't bet, you can't win. two: if you lose all your chips, you can't bet.

no right answer and YMMV... just food for thought 🍔

## 35. 2026-04-17
<a id="t-2045235071326835156"></a>

*Single post · [2045235071326835156](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2045235071326835156)*

thoughts on using this thread/article as a format -- esp for anyone older who is trying to transcribe or pass along what they've learned

"The Article of Articles" or "Master Thread."

7 things:

1. old wine, new bottles 

anyone from OG fintwit recognizes that this isnt really a new concept -- even back in <140 char tweets, people had "accumulated learnings"-style threads that they'd keep updating or adding to

articles are a huge step forward from that, plus you can edit them - you can see "last edited" so I avoid editing in general but for a Master Thread, its hugely helpful

2. motivation / pay-forwards

as a rule, the amount of doors that were opened for you when you were younger -- esp ones that you didn't deserve -- that's the amount of doors u must open in return

"you should do this" isn't always great motivation, but "you're ungrateful and disloyal if you DON'T do this" is pretty powerful

that's basically where I'm at, I had a ton of doors opened for me IRL and a ton of doors via FinTwit, none of which I deserved, so I'm 2x "wrong if I don't."

if u had great mentors, keep in mind a lot of ppl never were given that opportunity...werent born as lucky as u.

i'll let you decide if any of that applies to you or not

3. help yourself

other than cataloguing "what do I know" for your own sake, there's other benefits

-writing in general: sometimes putting stuff on paper lets you see that you dont understand it as well as u thought

-get feedback: putting your process out to the world, and letting them destroy it, is the fastest path to improvement / shredding "busted" principles

-best use of twtr: you can either try to fit in elsewhere, or you can put out your own energy and see who shows up. the latter takes longer but is far more rewarding

4. u arent important

on twtr and all social media, people are pretty self serving

none of us know one another, no one really cares who u are

"you are what you provide."

if you can help other people live a better life, or improve the way they think about markets, or $, or anything...they will read what u put out there

everything else about u is meaningless relative to that. and all criticism leveled ur way, is meaningless relative to who u can help.

5. on format + credit

the format in the article here, I don’t really know what I’m doing and I keep redoing the whole layout...twtr is pretty buggy, I'd love internal article links and a bunch of other upgrades but compared to the 140-char days, I cant complain

u can steal any/every part of it, I'm not a jerkoff about "give me credit"...others are, and so I give credit where due, but overall I believe in "its amazing what can get accomplished when u stop caring who gets credit."

the people who matter know who came first to shit, and more importantly, if u write authentically then no one can imitate you.

i'm not your competition and you're not mine. everything is in abundance. lots of envious mf'ers on here dont get that and view engagement as zero sum, when it never has been and never will be.

6. on topics

on topics, theres all sorts of shit in there - prob 50% markets, then the rest is life, human nature, or whatever comes to mind

I never have any idea what will do well and just keep trying to dig deeper and take risk

what is the endgoal...not sure. kind of just building lots of little sandcastles and then seeing what they look like

writing is a "game of tonnage" and so the tonnage has to come before u figure out what can be sculpted from it all.

7. fulfillment

once u get a decent catalog of articles, u have these assets that can help ppl in perpetuity

at any given time I have a handful of accounts in my notifications, where I can see that one by one, they're reading each article and clicking "like"

that in itself is insanely rewarding. when I was on here in 2013 or so, I was doing the same shit, but from scrolling an individual's feed...just soaking up everything they were willing to share.

big wheel keeps on turning, proud Mary keeps on burning

--

hth. remember:

"the best time to plant a tree was 20 years ago....the 2nd best time is today."

may as well get started

## 37. 2026-04-17
<a id="t-2045280633690222604"></a>

*Single post · [2045280633690222604](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2045280633690222604)*

back in 2014, Alice Schroeder (wrote "Snowball" about Buffett) did a Reddit AMA

"I was fortunate to have spent 10 years getting to know Warren Buffett by spending days and weeks with him."

some really good excerpts in there, I'll pick 8

1. on how little common equity investors know

"Before writing The Snowball, I was an analyst at several firms on Wall Street, a regulator, a CPA, and now I invest, and serve on corporate boards."

"I've been inside the sausage factory and know how complex business is in the real world. In that context, a lot of investor opinions display a high level of confidence based on a relatively sparse set of facts. 

So as you develop your skills its important to be mindful of the risk of overconfidence. This is where the margin of safety and circle of competence have done wonders for Warren. I would look at how he's applied those in investing and really think through the implications of "don't lose money.""

2. time management

"Warren is a master of time management. He knows how to ease people off the phone without making them feel dismissed. He is great at saying no and I learned a lot about saying no tactfully. That's an important time management technique. Also, he manages his energy, reading when it's optimal, talking on the phone when he's got the right energy for that and so forth. It's fairly compartmentalized and he does not multitask through his day."

3. valuing a company

"Warren's way is very simple. I have copies of an envelope or two where he did his calculation on the back - literally. If the margin of safety is wide enough, it almost doesn't matter what method you choose.

I know you want something more specific. I have to generalize, a lot, but hopefully this is helpful. Essentially it's a three step process. 

one: Is it an addressable investment? He rules out a lot of stuff that has too much tail risk, or he has no edge on the market. For example, thinking you can be smarter at buying Johnson & Johnson than everyone is essentially market timing. Warren rules that out unless he has some insight he is convinced no one else has. He may not always be right but it's the right approach and works over time. 

two: Downside protection. He looks for multiple ways to avoid losing money. If you look at the preferred stock deals he did after the financial crisis, he fenced in these companies and built in so many ways to avoid losing money it was almost funny. He will pass on huge upside opportunities if he can't get downside protection when its an investment as opposed to a bet such as March Madness deal. 

three: Capital generating power. His focus is on how much capital an investment can produce that can be reinvested either within the business or elsewhere. He prefers the easy route (reinvestment) however, ultimately he's agnostic, money is money and for a dollar he puts out he wants x% back. The x% has declined over the years with Berkshire's size and falling interest rates.

4. anecdote from 2004 / foresight

"Here's a good one. I bought a house in 2004 and when I told him he was aghast. That's when I knew we were in a serious housing bubble. Finally he said, well, don't worry, you'll be able to sell it in ten years, so just hold on until 2014."

5. re: "what is your favorite Buffett quote/memory?"

A: On who influences him: "When I get up in the morning I look in the mirror, and at that point, everybody's had their say."

6. on books/reading

"I like books from business history -- anything by Galbraith, Keynes, John Brooks. Mark Twain said history doesn't repeat but it rhymes and that's really true. Warren stressed to me over and over how much studying business history has helped him.

I would also recommend Generations: The History of America's Future by Strauss and Howe. or its follow up, The Fourth Turning.

Apart from books I think you should know what is going on that will affect the future. Not in the sense of forecasting. But for example, I was at a conference and asked a successful entrepreneur, "How do you get ideas to start businesses" since he had started quite a few. His answer ... "I look for big companies that have a lot of employees, and I figure out ways to get rid of the employees."

7. info filtering/process

"Warren does his reading in the morning. He had cataract surgery not long ago and it's made things much easier for him.

He starts with the newspapers. He looks at them page by page but doesn't read every article. He is very interested in news about companies and less interested in general news. Warren is outstanding at pattern recognition and prefers to do his own synthesis so when he reads he's looking more for data points rather than other people's conclusions.

The annual reports -- he reads them for the most part as they are received. He's been following some companies for fifty years or longer and as noted earlier he's got quite a memory, so the process of reading annual reports is pretty efficient for him. He can go through one in an hour or less and get what he needs. On the other hand, say when Google went public, he spent more time on their filings because it was new information. He reads a lot of annual reports of companies he would not buy to expand his knowledge.

The filtering is informal. His friends will send him something they think he will find interesting. Or he will read something in the paper and ask for the report. He isn't systematic nor comprehensive. That's today though. When he was younger, he filtered using Moody's, Value Line etc. reading everything about every company, then got the reports for the ones he was interested in. That's probably more relevant to you than what he does today.

Does he spend 500 hours. Well, he spends a lot of hours if it's something totally new. That said, Warren's investable universe is somewhat limited now by Berkshire's size which means he's already familiar with almost every investment. So he has probably invested that time, but it's cumulative over many years."

8. re: Weschler's "500 hours due diligence before making an investment"

"My understanding is that Ted's process is similar to Warren's and the amount of time sounds right. Let's say you work 50 hours a week, that's ten weeks of work. You could spend a large part of that time reading filings and articles about the company -- and its competitors -- and the industry. Then you do scuttlebutt on the people running the company, which can quite a bit of networking. Ted has a big network, I assume he works it for information. Then there is the garden variety information gathering such as channel checks, meetings with management, plant tours, visits to retail stores. Add to this the time spent every day reading relevant news, and the various conferences that investors attend. The valuation takes time -- early on you have a hypothesis and spend a lot of time testing and refining your assumptions. 500 hours is very reasonable.

The main effect of being in the sausage factory is to make me humble about the state of my knowledge. I am slower to pull the trigger and I pay attention to every bit of nuance I can find. My process is not that different from anyone else's. I do a few extra things. For example, it's useful to do a line by line comparison of press releases and filings over time to identify the "ins and outs." You can learn a lot from this. (Companies are trying to be more concise these days; even so it's amazing how often things disappear from sight when news is bad.) I also look at who is on the board and audit/risk committees. If you had looked at JP Morgan's risk committee composition along the way and seen the head of a museum on it who had no business experience, that was a signal. So I look at this sort of thing.

The advent of Reg FD has created a situation in which companies are forced to, essentially, lie to investors. So a company is not thinking about a divestment until the day it is suddenly announced. Loan loss reserves are adequate, and management has unshakeable confidence in that, until the day they are suddenly increased by a walloping amount. Inside the company, there are teams of accountants, consultants, bankers, executives and ultimately the board working on the projects that lead to these surprises. Most big events develop gradually over time. Companies used to do more signaling that helped investors adjust timely to changes rather than the present requirement to throw investors over the cliff. I think that in preventing companies from giving inside information to favored people, Reg FD pushed the system too far the other way."

--

last...a non-investing anecdote

"Dining with Warren is such an experience. Once, we were at a restaurant and Warren ordered a steak. The waiter offered him truffle sauce. The look on his face was priceless. it was as if someone said, would you like some arsenic, Mr. Buffett?"

## 38. 2026-04-18
<a id="t-2045421349699567910"></a>

*Single post · [2045421349699567910](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2045421349699567910)*

you know what's funny about this tweet... it's not entirely wrong. and I say that as a pretty "bookish" tweeter.

there is a balance between books and action.

(1/5)

one of the worst things you can be is "all books, no action."

the other extreme -- all action, no books -- is far better, if you had to pick one.

because action is rare, courage is rare. most people don't really do shit.

there's a lot of talkers. there's fewer readers, and there's even fewer "doers."

"no books + no action" prob covers the middle 90% of the bell curve.

(2/5)

and whereas reading can sort of be taught, its an old medieval truism that you'd rather have a knight who is too bold and too rash rather than one who is too timid.

the reason...you can teach restraint and discipline.

however, you cannot teach courage. 

you can't do shit with a timid knight. hence why the "all books, no action" type is relentlessly mocked throughout history.

Seneca shits all over these people, as does Montaigne - I referenced a few excerpts in the article below, on excessively quoting other authors. It's the same principle.

"men wearing other men's wisdom like a pearl necklace," or "a suit of armor where they're totally concealed."

this is 100% a type. you know this type, and they suck

(3/5)

previously I've written that two things change a person: books and experience (action). but you need both.

experience is what "unlocks" great books. failure, hardship, pain, suffering...without being fluent or at least conversational in these, much of what you read is off-limits.

even basic stoicism won't register. this is why I wouldn't recommend Seneca to a 21-year old -- you haven't suffered enough, it'll go over your head. come back in 15-20 years.

books are what contextualize hardship and pain, they help put guardrails around lived experience, and they ensure you aren't internalizing the wrong lessons.

but to keep reading, with zero experience and zero risks taken, is just mental masturbation.

(4/5)

experience is the best teacher. books shape experience... but books are subordinate to experience in the cap structure.

we are all unique. this is why you learn markets not from reading Buffett, but from losing a shitload of money. 

Buffett is one man. His temperament may be yours, or it may be the total opposite. He can steer you towards what works and he can help contextualize your losses, but they say "the market is a mirror" for a reason -- he can't make you know yourself.

this leads to another "type" that would be ridiculed by Seneca; the buffett-quoter. all books, no action, no original thoughts, no excess returns.

and so this same way that "you cant learn markets from books," that framework applies for everything, from love to entrepreneurship...starts and ends with action.

(5/5)

there is a reason why Aristotle, Plato, Aquinas and others all agree on the same principle: "Courage is the backbone of all virtues."

without courage, without a bias for action, all the knowledge and wisdom in your head sits there rotting away unused.

is david goggins well-read, no, but I will take his hard-won lessons from experience 100 out of 100 times over the excessive book quoter. or the "here's my hardship story, from when I got disillusioned with private equity."

i'd rather listen to clavicular. I doubt he can even read. but i'll learn more from his authentic life experience than from someone else's regurgitated wisdom.

how many "books guys" are there on twtr, who you sense have never put their ass on the line -- it's all pearl necklaces and suits of armor.

(END)

you'll see in the article below that seneca drills these types of people.

"marcus aurelius said this, nietzsche said this." 

"enough about what Epicurus said, or what Cleanthes said, or what Zeno said -- what do YOU say?? Put something forth from your own stock!"

but there's no stock to put forth. because without action...there is nothing to say.

audentes fortuna iuvat ⚔️

on "cheap virtue"

I keep going back to this article for diff reasons -- this is now the 3rd time -- because new parts of it, well, they just keep springing to mind.

here, it's what I guess I'd call cheap virtue. cheap entry to the game.

1/4

in mkts... that's the term I use for technicals

"cheap entry to the game"

guys see that it takes 10 years to learn fundamentals and a few months to learn technicals

they take the easy route

the market knows that, and it has nothing for ya...odd lots and two-bit piker dough...because it knows u havent paid dues (time) or tuition (losses).

so as a result u just spend years scalping bps and pips. 99% lose $, the rest come away flat or with "little-dick money."

2/4

in this article...I'll let u read it, but observe what "cheap virtue" the guy seeks to claim as to why he deserves good things:

"I'm a good son, good husband, law-abiding, I work hard, I help others..."

note that every man u have ever met, says those exact same things. default laundry list.

for some its true, for most it isnt.

but in all cases...it doesnt matter. because the world simply doesnt give a fuck.

u are what u provide.

3/4

this same idea, I'll list two other places you've heard it.

one, the brutal Glengarry Glen Ross speech: "Nice guy? I don't give a shit. Good father...fuck you, go home and play with your kids. You want to work here, you close."

its brutal because it captures what every man says after getting outcompeted, whether he just lost a super bowl or he's dialing for dollars.

back to the laundry list -- "at least I'm a good xyz" -- to try and dull the harsh, unforgiving pain of failure.

"...FUCK, I am SO fucking mad I lost."

it takes a big man to say the second part out loud.

you usually only hear the first part, the cope. here, it would be something like "Baldwin's an asshole anyway, I'd never want to be him."

4/4

the other "cheap virtue" comes courtesy of Chris Rock:

--

"A man will say some shit like “I take care of my kids.”

You’re supposed to, you dumb motherfucker! What are you talking about? What are you bragging about? Kinda ignorant shit is that?

“I ain’t never been to jail!” What you want, a cookie? You’re not supposed to go to jail, you low-expectation-having motherfucker!"

--

again, just brutal -- both brilliant and brutal -- in that the traits a man holds dear, his final sources of comfort when all else has been lost, are ripped into pieces right in front of his eyes.

because this world, it has no use for your cheap virtue.

it cares about one thing and one thing only: what it can get from you.

that's the article. enjoy

## 43. 2026-04-21
<a id="t-2046575204290248995"></a>

*Single post · [2046575204290248995](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2046575204290248995)*

on risk mgmt, trading, stop-losses, and products/instruments

this is a quick post on cutting losses, mostly aimed at ppl who are younger and trading "dogshit" for lack of a better word.

i.e. crypto/shitcoins/gambling/prediction markets

no offense. these are dogshit for risk mgmt, and by Buffett's rules, all worthless.

Buffett 101: if it doesnt produce cash flow, it has no intrinsic value.

I would use that as a hard-and-fast rule for what to trade or invest in.  no cash flow = no go slash dogshit.

if you agree, keep reading. 

if not, I cant help you...if you wont listen to the GOAT, you sure as hell wont listen to me.

--

(1/5)

I would strongly encourage leaving behind all non-cash flowing products and picking up either equities (like Mag7), index ETFs (SPY), or S&P futures (1 lots of the tiniest contract).

the #1 of these would be S&P futures, which are the focus of this post.

the reason is that S&P futures are the best instrument for learning the basics of trading and risk mgmt, by minimizing gap risk.

like I said - this is written from the angle of responsible advice: "upgrade from dogshit to a real product." 

(2/5)

I say this just to allay any pushback along the lines of: "the responsible advice is to tell people to buy and hold the S&P 500 index."

yes, it is. people know that, and they still want to trade. 

so if they're going to trade, the transition away from shitcoins or Polymarket or sports gambling, to S&P futures, is a massive upgrade from every possible angle -- especially risk mgmt.

I am confident that this post will save at least one blown account, and given that many blown accounts are followed by someone blowing their brains out, you never know.

that's what this post is, nothing more. 

you cannot succeed as an equity investor if you do not know how to cut losses.

(3/5)

S&P futures are a grown man's game. leave all this other shit behind, the coins, betting on the Ayatollah dying or whatever people are doing.

prediction markets are inefficient, but not in your favor, the "edge" is being captured by Qatar, by organized crime syndicates, by Russian oligarchs...you have no informational edge and are crazy to think that you do.

trade S&P 500 futures, indices, and U.S. listed equities -- and nothing else -- from now until you die.

look at the fortune 500. this is how everyone gets rich. these are the products buffett, munger, and the rest of them use. 

final destination. grown man's game.

if ur friends want to gamble then let them gamble...u have to choose between "do I want to make money" or "do I want to get high from gambling."

this message sounds blunt but you'd be amazed how many people will read Buffett, look at the Fortune 500, see that they all got rich from U.S. equities....and then they'll go blow an account trading 50x levered monkeycoins.

4/5: on futures

liquidity will never be an issue, your stop will always fill at the nearest tick, save for gap risk (weekends and a short pause after 4 PM close) - they will probably have 24/7 versions of this soon, if they don't already, so my knowledge may be stale.

if not, S&P futures (ES) micros or supermicros or whatever there is now are your best bet.

by trading ES and reading tape/news/headlines, you are gaining real reps in a fundamentally-driven asset, not some stupid manipulated shitcoin which trades with 0 true correlation to anything...those are empty reps, empty calories.

your time spent trading ES compounds. it will carry over to single stocks, because price reacts to news the same way in all US-listed equities. 

the price/news/price/news feedback loop is what you only learn thru these reps and long hours.

5/5 on risk:

the biggest thing to me is "muscle memory." 

you want to get intimately familiar with cutting losses and getting stopped out.

this is where new investors struggle. they are not fluent in cutting losses.

its an itchy trigger finger, that's the muscle.

you want to get comfortable cutting risk and exiting positions, immediately, no hesitation

bad investors/traders freeze up when the P&L turns red and forget to cut. 

this is how you blow your "down 10%" stop and then just sit there as it goes down 50%. happens to everyone.

once you blow the first stop, you have no stop.

by trading 1 lots of ES over and over, and focusing on honing this exact skill -- hair-trigger exits -- you won't panic when the heat is on.

FINAL ADVICE ON RISK

remember, great traders don't always exit for a reason. 

if it feels wrong, EXIT.

if you aren't sure why you have the position on, EXIT.

if you expected the market to react a certain way on a headline, and it's flat or moving against you, get the fuck out of the trade.

don't sit there having risk on just watching shit wiggle around for no reason, hoping it goes your way.

if you have no view or no trade plan, don't put $ at risk.

these are all rookie/mid-level mistakes and thought patterns that have to be wiped clean if you ever want to have a shot at being great

if you can't sit there watching tape for hours, without transacting and with zero risk on, you don't have the patience to succeed

people who are monkeys for action, gamblers, people looking to get high by having risk on...they will never make it.

this is why S&P futures are suitable: you are training all the correct mental and emotional "muscles." 

if you want to add individual equities, great, that's the long-term goal...just be aware that you have daily gap risk plus potential liquidity risk (getting a bad fill on the stop-out).

TL;DR: risk management comes first, learning to cut losses comes first, and you cannot succeed in fundamental equity investing if you do not know how to take a loss.

--

that's all I got.

if you are a professional and see something I mis-stated or could have phrased better, fire away.

good luck trading

## 47. 2026-04-24
<a id="t-2047760417678577939"></a>

*Single post · [2047760417678577939](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2047760417678577939)*

the best path to getting tailored feedback and criticism:

updating priors

usually I've just said "write a bunch, do stock pitches, post them, take the twtr feedback and improve on it"

this advice is insufficient...not gonna get u what u need.

too important to ignore - entire process needs a top-to-bottom refresh

(1/7)

for a tweet to get enough feedback where there's signal in the noise, you need an absolute fuckton of engagement

I don't even think 100k, its probably 250k-500k followers where you've got a list of replies and feedback on the replies

Until then, you have normal engagement signals (good/bad post), but no real tailored feedback on your work that you can improve off

Also, it's rare that someone shows up out of nowhere and puts time into their feedback...almost never happens. 

Usually it's one guy with a "thanks" and another guy calling you a cuck...someone occasionally writes one of those posts where they clearly didn't read yours, just saw "oh, its about multiples and DCF" and shares why they prefer multiples to DCFs

So, I gotta nix "just tweet and see what happens" as procedure. I look at accounts 10x larger than mine, they still don't have the volume of replies necessary for that to be your sole feedback mechanism.

(2/7)

Best path I think is to find 5 people whose opinion you really trust in assessing the quality of your work

There's a term my friend uses which is lateral to this - "Personal Board of Directors"

That's not the right group here, but it's something to think about as you're seeking ppl out...you're never too old to find new mentors and advisors.

So for criticism, even 3 is probably fine, but you gotta let them know...like:

"I need 3 consistent Road Dogs - people I can bounce shit off once every week or two, to tear my work down, give harsh criticism, and blast holes in my ideas."

And then get on a schedule, like same time every week I'm coming, you know its coming...I'm not going to put demands on your time but if you know its coming Fridays at noon, chances are you're gonna turn it around quick.

(3/7)

Diversify the group, don't ask just stock market dorks...mix real life/Internet, the more diverse the group of opinions is, the more different angles you'll see in which thru which ppl interpret ur writing

Push the envelope on the shit you write about, if someone is a great creative writer don't send them a stock pitch, send them something wild and out of bounds....they're not judging you.

People are universally helpful in asks like this for several reasons, most importantly being that everyone wants feedback, and you must give to receive. 

But also, you're not doing what everyone else does - pop out of nowhere - "hey man whats up, heres a PDF, can you gimme feedback."

Plop a turd in my DM and skate off

I'll do it, but I'm also just as likely to say "ok," get back to what I was doing and then genuinely forget

(4/7)

So if I at least know, alright, Wednesday at some point this guy will want help, it feels less intrusive

Also, the more specific you can be on what you want, the more that helps everyone. 

My first feedback is always going to be formatting, fucked-up decimal places, centering, unprofessional-looking model output

I don't know why, I think it's a default to "if you can't do the little shit right, you can't do the big shit right" as an attitude. Which, frankly, is true. 

(5/7)

And then I'll do a 30-second scan of the 1st page and 2nd page, just bc that's what most people will do

Is this official, does it pass the "smell test," is there a price target, numbers vs. consensus

Like is this a pitch-ready pitch to critique, or a "Your pitch format needs critique" pitch

I can do both but if it fails the smell test, the content is moot

Those other 15 pages, I'm not touching them. The first 1-2 have to be sound for people to go any further. Those are the pitch, the rest is support for what was written

But maybe none if this is what you wanted feedback on, so I don't know - these are wasted minutes for both of us.

(6/7)

It's not on THEM - it's on YOU, the person asking for feedback, to make this entire process seamless

No matter how wise someone is, a document is a document, there's a lot of angles - you want proofreading, content, formatting, style, you want me to cut the word count in half, or tell you where to replace words with tables? 

Challenge your assumptions, math, choice of valuation method, ests vs street, primary research...there's no upper limit as far as what you can ask someone to destroy.

So If I don't get anything, I'll just send links to old pitches of mine and say "man use this format and come back"

(7/7)

Last, not all people have equal feedback fidelity...you'll see that some people consistently unearth gold, others will catch a few typos

I have one friend in real life, prob 15-20 years at this point, a blessing - I've sent him long-ass shit to read and he just processes it and comes back "this is actually your title, this is your real theme, this is what your summary page is, first line should be X."

Always just smashes it dead-on

But nothing is perfect. Because while I know he's going come back with two or three piss missiles that were exactly what I'm looking for in feedback and criticism, he has the same background I do...so forest/trees becomes an issue.

Tailoring your writing around what some L/S equity guy would like, which is probably boring as fuck to the masses.

So its kind of just knowing the strengths of each person, and most importantly, where their "feel" for the crowd may be off.

END

You really only need 3 really high-signal humans. Maybe 5.

The issue with too large a number, is that you start getting conflicting opinions, or you ask five people and get five answers. All while your gut probably knows better than any of them.

Ideally you want someone who reads wide and deep, novels, history, biography, these people will unsurprisingly shine the brightest light. Because they know what pro-grade shit is supposed to look like.

Are there a lot of these people, not really. Some people "read" but it doesn't fully upload and integrate the way it does for others.

Would you want feedback from Munger, hell yeah. These multidisciplinary thinkers and polymath-types are probably going to give the criticism with the highest signal in it.

But the most important thing, is that figuring out the strengths and weaknesses of each person, and configuring your entire approach around it...that's on YOU. 

/end priors update

## 48. 2026-04-25
<a id="t-2048006599306887615"></a>

*Single post · [2048006599306887615](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2048006599306887615)*

10 little things that have helped me in writing:

1. the gap between 90% and 100% only exists in your head 

done is better than perfect

twtr doesn't have to be perfect.  if it's 90% done, hit it and move on...its 100% to the crowd.

2. early AM weekends (4-8 AM)

only time u can get guaranteed quiet, peace, and productivity. 

3. never look back

after its out, its out - property of the world, no longer urs.

never revise, never reread. only opens the door to negative habits and emotions --nitpicking, cringe, regret, self-doubt, shame -- waste of time.

if u are rapidly improving as a writer, stuff u wrote 3 months ago will make u want to vomit. thats progress.

4.  Write the first page last

write first. figure out what u wrote later. after u do it this way u will realize that every other approach is a poor use of time, inefficient, prob reduces quality as well from choppiness

5. sprint towards fear 

similarly, if u start by asking - "what is everyone else terrified to write about?" - these are the best answers.

shame, guilt, insecurity, cowardice, the world discovering that u have absolutely no clue what ur doing

people are going to want to read what u, or anyone, have to say on these dark dirty corners of the soul.

6. sprint towards darkness

people are way weirder and sicker than u think.

when in doubt, always go as dark and bizarre and strange as possible

its just less crowded first of all. who is writing about drug addiction, or asking if anyone wants to join a new jazz band called "the registered sax offenders 🎷"

I have a draft article quoting bukowski: "if you want to find out who your real friends are, get a jail sentence" 

anything that others shy away from...sensitive, hairy, requires delicate touch...i'd go there.

7. courage is its own reward

if it was hard to write, it will be well-recieved. ppl recognize things that are hard to say and do.

usually accountability, ownership, unpopular views

8. speak truth to pain

pain always resonates for some reason. u have to think back to extreme pain that u felt, what caused it, and then find a way to get it onto the page with that same emotion

starting with these, is going to give u fertile ground and better odds of success than anything else

"12 prompts for claude" vs "12 times I disgraced my family"

9. emotional writer, emotional reader

if u felt something as it went onto the page, they are going to feel it on the uptake

I can't explain why, it just is. I'll have to pull a quote from a book I read recently on Strindberg, its an old writers technique to become genuinely emotional one way or another before u sit down to write

it just bleeds out onto the page

10. sprint away from comfortable

if it feels easy and comfy, its played out

never be the 500th guy writing about "how to fix ur life in 3 seconds" or "the 2.13 rules of wealth" or anything LLM

would you click on an article called "why software stocks are oversold?"

or...would you click on this one?

"10 reasons why the valley's top VC's can't stop jerking off with gorilla glue"

no brainer.

now go be great my friend✍️
