## 49. 2026-04-28
<a id="t-2049104122650022058"></a>

*Single post · [2049104122650022058](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2049104122650022058)*

on adverse outcomes, managing expectations, negative visualization... 

"bad news is preferable to uncertainty"

unpleasant lifelong struggle that can't be wrapped up in a bow -- no neat conclusion.

1/5

easiest example that everyone has been familiar with at some point or another...

waiting for outcome of job intvw. uncertain timing (0-2 weeks)...zero ability on your end to influence outcome...but where ur near-term planning is thrown into flux

ur hopeful, but do u proceed as if u have nothing? u know u should, yet hope is a motherfucker

this dynamic doesnt change as u rise in seniority or hang ur own shingle, just diff forms - "everybody's gotta serve somebody" as dylan put it

waiting on response from a potential hire or customer or LP, or legal/compliance, but mostly learning over and over that "everything is sales."

2/5

but for job intvw I see younger ppl fall into the same trap, of "they said theyll get back to me in 3 weeks" and then they stop looking...then when it doesnt go their way, they're flat-footed and learn that time was wasted

at the same time its not straightforward because social capital is limited in a sense. theres nothing wrong with staying in front of ppl, and I think u can always err in that direction because we've all been in that position.

so ur both hopeful, braced for negative outcomes and unsure how to proceed

3/5

the exercise to get used to, imo, is negative visualization

this is old shit, dates back to the Greeks -- embracing future bad news so that the impact is dampened if/when it arrives

so u have to sit there and imagine the email arriving:

"REJECTED."

feel it, absorb it, visualize the scenario in real-time

and then lay out the range of outcomes - what do u do when that arrives?

so this way u can immediately spring into action (vs being flat-footed/emotionally blasted) and keep the ball rolling

lick ur wounds later, in private, after all that is done. the point is not to waste time which is the only thing u never get back

4/5

this applies in trading mkts obviously, where u have no pre mortem, no stop, no "what does it look like if I'm wrong" scenario thought out in advance

so the headline hits, it moves down 10%, and instead of taking the loss (as u objectively should) ur brain springs into action and every cognitive bias in the book activates against u

10% becomes 20%...u know the rest. failure comes from not preparing for failure

5/5

the correct path I think is to just mold urself into someone who immediately embraces all "nightmare" scenarios, visualizes them, blunts any future emotional impact, and has an decision tree to execute upon arrival

this gets tricky pending ur personality type tho, because not everyone has the constitution to operate this way...very easy to slip into "nothing ever goes my way," self-defeating behavior, negative feedback loops

why its so rare to find someone who fits the Churchill'ism on how success = moving from failure to failure without losing enthusiasm 

END

and so this ties into the market truism - "markets can handle bad news, they cant handle uncertainty" - given that markets are driven by human nature, psychology, fear/greed and all of that, shouldnt surprise anyone that this is an individual truism as well

unfortunately I dont have a neatly-wrapped solution or conclusion for u...personally I have made so many mistakes that they feel like the natural rhythm of life to me, own it, fix it, make sure it doesnt happen again, come back stronger

but uncertainty, fucks up the entire rhythm for me...esp as a type A, hard-charging, internal locus, obsessive control freak who is actively pushing the other direction in search of the golden mean ("control what u can control," lay back, let God take the wheel, etc)

mistakes dont keep me up at night, but uncertainty has u waking up at 3 am like "what more can I do to positively influence this outcome"...when u know full well there isnt shit.

--

hth / inevitably more to follow on this "Q with no A."

## 55. 2026-05-11
<a id="t-2053774558340026738"></a>

*Single post · [2053774558340026738](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2053774558340026738)*

Dan Benton's "20 Rules for Technology Investing" - 1991 & 2026 Editions

1991 Edition:

--

Momentum

1. Sell technology stocks when estimates are being reduced.

2. Buy technology stocks only for positive earnings surprises.

3. Positive earnings surprises occur when revenue and earnings growth are accelerating, when average selling prices are rising, and when gross margin and operating margin are rising.

4. Most technology stock ideas are product-cycle stories.

5. New product cycles often lead to earnings surprises; product-cycle transitions usually lead to earnings disappointments.

6. Technology stocks also do well when companies rebound from periods of poor execution.

Valuation

7. Value investors don’t make money in technology. There are few “cheap” technology stocks.

8. Don’t buy on relative P/E, P/B, P/R, particularly when estimates are falling.

Seasonality

9. Technology stocks perform poorly in the summer.

10. Seasonal slowdowns cause secular concerns.

11. Second-tier companies perform worst in the weakest seasonal periods and provide anecdotal evidence of an industry slowdown.

Management

12. Reorganizations without restructuring charges usually lead to earnings disappointments within two quarters.

13. One-quarter problems exist (but only if caused by supply constraints).

14. Management usually appears weakest at the bottom of a product cycle.

15. Insider selling doesn’t matter; management receives new stock options every year.

Old World/New World

16. Traditional mainframe and minicomputer companies are in secular decline.

17. It is increasingly difficult to differentiate companies that sell microprocessor-based computers.

18. Execution is the most important distinguishing factor in a standards-based world.

19. It is hard to forecast execution.

20. Don’t forget Rule 1.

--

2026 Edition:

1. Don’t trade technology stocks. Invest in secular themes.

2. Secular growth overpowers economic cycles.

3. But don’t fight the Fed. Technology is now the market.

Momentum

4. Secular growth drives acceleration.

5. Product cycles, platform extensions, and geographic expansion also accelerate growth.

6. Revenue acceleration creates operating margin leverage.

7. Technology stocks move in the direction of estimates. Forecast at least two years out.

8. Invest when your estimates are out of consensus and correct.

Valuation

9. Valuation doesn’t matter to momentum stocks.

10. When growth slows, they become GARP stocks and multiples compress.

11. Seasonality is a distraction. Forget “sell in May and go away.”

12. Cheap technology stocks usually aren’t. Most turnarounds fail.

13. Two rocks tied together don’t make a raft, but tech M&A is a risk to a short.

14. If a short beats numbers for two quarters in a row, you were wrong. It was a long.

15. Cover shorts when they stop declining on bad news.

Management

16. All managements try to underpromise. Only the best consistently overdeliver.

17. Mature companies blame the economy or the dollar.

18. Stock-based compensation is an expense. Buybacks to offset dilution create no value.

19. Insider selling doesn’t matter. Buyback announcements accompanied by disappointing outlooks don’t either.

Summary

20. The market is faster than it was 35 years ago. But it’s not smarter. See Rule 1.

--

(source: @danbenton)

---

## 56. 2026-05-11
<a id="t-2053805642637296102"></a>

*Single post · [2053805642637296102](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2053805642637296102)*

two important charts & tables from Mauboussin in April 2024 ("Valuation Multiples") on capital intensity and understanding EV/EBITDA multiples

I'll try and break it all down further & make practical

1. EBITDA, capex, depreciation factors

-the first photo has sectors sorted by capital intensity

-this is done by breaking EBITDA into EBIT and DA

-DA here is used as a proxy for maintenance capex, so higher DA = higher capital intensity = lower "biz quality" = lower multiple.

-highest capital intensity sectors: utilities, telecom, energy

-lowest capital intensity sectors: consumer, information tech

-the ratio of EBITDA to EBIT is labeled as "depreciation factor" here -- essentially, the "low capital intensity/low depreciation factor" businesses have lower reinvestment needs, require less ongoing $$ to grow ---> should convert more EPS to FCF (capex being the primary driver of IS vs. CF differences) ---> higher "biz quality" ---> higher multiple.

-utilities have a 1.8 depreciation factor, consumer 1.3

-higher depreciation factor tends to = the stock trades at a lower multiple. this is because high capital intensity weighs on ROIC. the denominator, IC ("invested capital") goes up, since growth requires more capital to be invested.

-this tightens the (ROIC - WACC) spread, which is what drives multiples.

-put another way, more revenue is "eaten up" by DA on the way down to NI and then FCF in a biz w/ high capital intensity.

2. EV/EBITDA multiples based on depreciation factor

-second photo has three tables: depreciation factors of 1.2, 1.4, and 1.6

-remember that higher depreciation factor = higher capital intensity = "lower quality" biz = lower multiple

-each table has growth vs ROIC

-WACC = 7.6%

-takeaway 1: growth below this 7.6% WACC destroys value ---> lower multiple from reinvesting below cost of capital, which creates negative (ROIC - WACC) spread ---> value gets destroyed.

-takeaway 2: higher depreciation factor (higher capital intensity) --> lower multiples in all three tables.

---

if this is unclear reply/DM with questions...this definitely wasn't clear to me until I had 5+ years of experience but is critical to understand. P/E and EPS tell you very little -- FCF, reinvestment needs, (ROIC - WACC) are what drive valuation & asset prices.

---

recommended reading

Erasmus: "Adagia"

this was a real good book…I broke most of it down in the article but to sum up what makes a lot of the best books, the best books, is that they are designed to arm u with precepts and maxims that can be recalled as-needed in helping u navigate life/relationships/human nature

there really aren’t any GAAP (generally accepted armed precepts?) - and the truth is that across many old books, u will see contradictory precepts or proverbs... yet that are both true. the Bible is full of these

and so which precept is “correct,” like many things, comes down to knowledge of self -- what works for YOU, your temperament, etc -- no different than all the different schools of thought in trading markets (Buffett/Druck/Simons are all different yet all "correct").

as for this book, what I can say is that some 3 months later, I definitely have found much of it springing to mind at opportune times…the precepts stuck, and they have continued to make themselves available for recall.

Erasmus is more a "compiler" than anything, many of the best precepts in here come courtesy of Seneca or Plutarch. It doesn’t matter to me whether he compiled them or invented them, all that matters is that they plant themselves in the fertile soil of the mind, the subconscious…and so I owe Eramus (as well as the author of this recent translation) a debt of gratitude.

you can prob skip my meandering nonsense at the beginning of this article and scroll down, I pulled 20 excerpts directly from the text...4 random screenshots below...enjoy.

## 58. 2026-05-13
<a id="t-2054538234282000563"></a>

*Single post · [2054538234282000563](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2054538234282000563)*

re: ROIC/WACC, reinvestment, cost of equity

sometimes I DM with ppl who are sharp, autodidacts, individual investors who know their shit (prob an engineer/lawyer/doctor type), way smarter than me but who get extremely academic with a lot of their approach

this can be problematic 

(1/3)

what I would point out is the gap between theory and practice...academia and arcana, so to speak

these excerpts from Buffett and Munger I think are the best way to illustrate this:

"I’ve listened to many cost of capital discussions and they’ve never made much sense. It’s taught in business school and consultants use it, so Board members nod their heads without any idea of what’s going on.”

"The concept of hurdle rates makes nothing but sense, but it doesn’t work."

they are as skeptical as it gets on this kind of stuff. are they correct, who knows, but they're the closest we've got to an arbiter of truth in investing and markets

you can ctrl+f in the article below to a section "How Buffett and Munger Discount Their Cash Flows," I stuffed as many quotes as I could find in there. 

(2/3)

cost of debt is somewhat observable, but cost of equity...that's really a made-up number.

"It’s easy to figure out your cost of borrowing, but theorists went bonkers on the cost of equity capital.”

ties in w/ their notion that "having too high an IQ is bad for trading markets."

I say this bc some people in DMs get overly precise on WACC or cost of equity, and overly focused on "is ROIC >WACC?"

and the truth is that if you ask a seasoned professional, (ie not me, someone way smarter/sharper), they'll prob co-sign on "yeah a lot of this is imprecise."

this all makes it tricky and frustrating for someone trying to learn investing and markets...the truth tends to be ineffable, and unlike science, markets in practice for some reason NEVER seem to mirror markets in theory. don't ask me why.

(3/3)

in that last tweet I put up from Mauboussin, there were tables where WACC was 7.74%, and so ROIC in excess of that creates value, below it destroys value...and so people recognize that, and get overly precise in their models

but its really best thought of as high-level theory.

if your model is precise to 7.50% vs 8.00%, as far was whether ROIC > WACC...its probably too precise, to where the inputs have become useless.

same for DCF's, where a 100 bp chg in discount rate has a huge effect and frustrates ppl...but a DCF isnt meant to be precise.

this is Buffett 101 where "if it isnt obvious, screw it." the range of outcomes for ROIC can be wide, but say 10-15%, where in all circumstances its >WACC (of 7.7%).

if the yes/no hinges on the 100 bp discount rate delta in a DCF, its probably a "no."

so on all this, there are practitioners like Damodaran who are worth reading, but who (in my opinion) fall short of Mauboussin in connecting 1. theory, to 2. how markets actually price assets in real-time.

--

END

if I were trying to ELI5 all of this ("explain like I'm 5") I think the best path is copy all the buffett/munger material from this article, paste into LLM's, and just keep hitting them until you understand that the truth is somewhere in the middle of theory and practice.

buffettfaq dot com has plenty as well

I wish I had a better answer for you, but tbh, I'm still trying to figure this all out myself.

good luck trading

> QT @gregoryblotnick: "Lazy Thinking" in Equity Valuation (Part 3): DCFs + Climbing Mount Stupid

## 59. 2026-05-15
<a id="t-2055342178084278525"></a>

*Single post · [2055342178084278525](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2055342178084278525)*

from this transcript, the thing that stayed w/ me most is how often Todd Combs frames everything in confidence intervals

1. "what are earnings in 5 years, at a 90% confidence interval"

2. "how many co's will compound at 7%, at a 50% confidence interval"

3. "where is this company in its lifecycle using xyz confidence interval"

this is really the correct way to think about anything in markets, where everything is just a range of outcomes and probability weightings...nothing is certain, nothing is discrete

this framework takes time to get used to, there was a tweet a while back from a random account with a transcript of Peter Thiel talking about some company, and the tweet had a lot of engagement along the lines of "why does he talk about investing like this?"

it took me a little to see what they were talking about. blind spots like a motherfucker

its definitely a public markets thing where you constantly appear uncertain, and hedge all your statements, but thats the way it goes, and its consonant with how assets are priced

i'll go try and dig the tweet up now but I just wanted to highlight the confidence interval language, I feel like its not as common as it should be + is a term I'll prob lean on more in the future.

## 60. 2026-05-16
<a id="t-2055647698590511542"></a>

*Thread · 2 posts · conversation `2055647698590511542`*

### 1/2

*2026-05-16 · [2055647698590511542](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2055647698590511542)*

on historical chart patterns, market mechanics, and "the pool"... loose scribblings

one thing I'd point out -- and I'll lay out how u can verify this on ur own -- is that markets never go from "correction" to "bubble" in a month

it just doesn't work that way

1/9 - grinding charts

there's arcana & non-fundamental variables that must be weighed

I have no view or bias here fwiw, this is merely an observation that may help u in assessing what inning we're in + the duration of this rally

the starting point of this analysis is to pull up a daily candlestick chart of SPX (tradingview is good) and just scroll backwards 50 years or so, uploading it all to your brain...take ur time, only gotta do it once

1987 is a decent endpoint

some things in markets never change, yet at the same time, a chart pattern from 1883 should be assigned less weight...regimes change, SPX weightings change, some things do change.

there's a cutoff somewhere. we can debate if its 50, 100 or 150 years, but I think 1987 + understanding the inflationary 1970's regime + leads/lags of a hike cycle, is sufficient

2/9 - trough to peak moves

now...what u are looking for, is all the local lows, selloffs and corrections, as well as understanding what drove them.

using just SPX/ES is fine. nasdaq/NQ chart is really a higher-beta version, plus, u want to stay open to a scenario where $ flows from secular to cyclical...NQ down, ES flat, rotation, ES shrugs off NQ weakness to print new highs.

you'll get the info u need here from SPX

for each selloff or correction, u want to note the duration of the rally that followed

there is a lot of bubble talk lately, which is to be expected when u had max bearish positioning on 3/31 followed by ES ripping 1200 handles in a straight line, without any clear "step in here" or overhang removal

ppl aren't positioned and understandably so. tricky tape. but...generally, u dont hear "its a bubble" from people who are long

3/9 - Pusha T: "Reagan Era, I Ran Contraband"

worth keeping in mind:

"no one is more bearish than a sold out bull"

so u want to at least do ur own due diligence. draw ur own conclusion from historical patterns + grinding charts

in both april 2026 and april 2025, the crowd has been a reliable contra...max bearish at both lows, missed both rallies

again, I have no strong view and I usually ignore twtr, but if ur whole feed says "bubble," and u feel as if the contra RSQ is -1, I'd at least scribble a few notes in the sentiment/positioning folder

as someone who has prob spent ~33% of his life in front of a bbg tape or grinding charts, I respectfully submit that over the past 50 years, I have never seen a market go from "correction" to "bubble," and when I say bubble I mean in the sense of imminent danger

4/9 - the pool

the Nasdaq was trading 1000 on 1/1/98

were people calling it a bubble then, sure...and in 97 and 96, just like ppl saw subprime in 05

by the end of 1999, NQ had 4x'ed...traded just shy of 4000

but what u gotta keep in mind is that in summer 1998, SPX drew down 20%

this is what I'm getting at.

enter "the pool"

5/9 - hope floats

sometimes the silliest and simplest images describe market arcana the best

"everyone in the pool" or "everyone out of the pool"

when everyone has gotten out of the pool, the market is sold out. it can bounce along the lows but there's no one left...the next move is driven by everyone getting back in the pool

tops are trickier, but similarly, u do tend to have everyone in the pool

if ur someone who has watched tape for decades u know what I mean re: buoyancy...u sort of just see in px action, which is why the pool analogy is a good one

6/9 - reset positioning

obviously early 2000 is a full pool, but another decent analog is the 2021 rally

everyone had gotten in the pool

in mid-1998, the pool emptied out

that's what provides the fuel for the 12-24 mo rallies that follow corrections...sentiment/positioning gets reset.

understanding this has to take precedent over whatever fundamental concerns or signs of froth u point to

also, pointing to signs of froth when some 40 days ago we were on the brink of WW3...people have real short memories

7/9 - respect the mechanics

but there are market mechanics that have to be respected, mechanics being = the pool emptying & filling back up

that shit takes time

I dont even care how stupid it sounds to type "u gotta fill the pool" bc ppl who have been doing this for a long time know how goddamn stupid mkts can be, and that the simplest and stupidest-sounding answers are often the most accurate

"GS prime brokerage data says this, but MS says this, and L/S grosses are here and nets are there and CTA's flip long at 7450 and BAML's institutional manager survey says overweight cash."

bro

the pool just emptied

the pool must refill

a bubble cannot pop when the pool is still empty

go grind charts, draw ur own conclusions, push back if I'm wrong. I'm wrong all the damn time

but the timeline for empty pool ---> danger zone, is not 1 month. gun to my head its 12-18 months...not making a call, just looking back at the duration of rallies following 10-20% corrections

8/9 - the repositioning

"why does it take so long"

sometimes flows take center stage and nothing else matters... every fundamentally-trained guy comes to the realization "druck is prob right that liquidity/flows/monpol drives everything." 

so u have 3-6 months where all the yo-yos who sold NQ 23k now add length at 29k, the various bots and quants and algos and CTAs whose model registered a "time to buy" signal (1200 handles tends to do that) must now buy

that's why I say "mechanical," even L/S has shifted discretionary --> mechanical in nature

pointless to speculate "who exactly is buying," waste of time, always just blind guesses...u just know that positioning was offsides, u know that "markets always move to cause the most pain to the most ppl" and u see the tape not letting anyone in

9/9 - lifecycle of a trade

sometimes shit goes up really fast without it being bearish. ppl have a hard time with that for some reason, like they see the market come roaring off oversold conditions, they see "nvda prints new all-time high," and they say: "holy fuck thats SO bearish for nvda."

like where do u get that read lol. did it go up too fast or something...is this burry worship, where r his puts marked

ultimately, none of us are druck, but I'm going to link to this article where I put some excerpts from him and his NVDA trade

the same reads he's making there, are just what it looks like when u have someone who is a gifted tape reader...can sense flows/positioning and can clearly apply that framework to an individual stock (NVDA) re: "what inning is this in"

bc he knows that earnings revisions are lagging indicators vs identifying: 1. this story is 1st/2nd inning 2. the magnitude of this narrative shift change is not reflected in street ests or how ppl are positioned 3. the "pool" in this stock is still empty.

gunned it from $150 to $390, doubled down 2x at higher prices, then took the trade at $800

man gives u his playbook...he open sources the game, bc he knows full well that there's no risk in doing so:

"none of these motherfuckers can execute on this shit the way I do." 

--

hth & USA (use stops always)

## 61. 2026-05-16
<a id="t-2055715658256576541"></a>

*Single post · [2055715658256576541](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2055715658256576541)*

re: primary research

forgot I had written this screenshot, someone found it helpful

to be honest though, I feel like this is a part of the business that has gotten nerfed

(1/5)

everyone being forced to use the same "compliance-approved primary research" amounts to a farce, a jerkoff

ppl going thru the motions to have some justification for their ests...all while knowing that if every other L/S guy knows what u know, its priced.

15 years ago my boss would say "for the next month u are going to drive around visiting every single Pep Boys from Boston to Philly. Talk to every store manager, ask them these q's, document everything, don't come back until you've done at least 100 stores."

was there any edge in this, hard to say, bc they have 700 other stores that I didn't visit...the margin of error is wide.

but that at least is what primary rsch has always been & is supposed to be

perhaps I'm nostalgic and overly sentimental but this felt like one of the "old school" aspects of investment research, shoe leather, scuttlebutt, all these archaic terms for what appears to be a dying art

and in practice, there's a real chance of a sharp analyst picking something up from visiting 100 stores. some shift in culture, insight on mgmt, on product, on whether the employees give a shit.

(2/5)

the timing is uncertain but none of that stuff is reflected in street ests

u would be amazed how many people will just blindly buy some shit like a SOTP-driven Family Dollar long pitch and make it a 7% position.

"you ever been to one, dude?"

"no, but it's priced like a call option, there's barely any value ascribed to it in EV...recession-resistant biz, white space opportunity"

that in itself can't form a short thesis, yet at the same time, hard to think of a better signpost than "bulls have no idea what they own"

u know why its priced like a call option? 

because EBIT is declining 50% and the stores look like 1942 Stalingrad

all u had to do was visit a store and ur thesis was blown

draft memo..."long high-quality biz at depressed multiple"...right in the trash

depressed multiple isn't half as depressed as the business

(3/5)

you look at your original pitch:

"if FDO is valued at 4x EBITDA, there's 50% upside"

then u walk inside and realize that 1x would be generous...and that the 1x, that's a 1x DA multiple, there's no EBIT. straight DA, 1x Adjusted Non-GAAP DA, which becomes 10x Adj. DA after factoring in operating lease debt and an underfunded pension

ppl don't even visit 1 store. and when they do, its corp access/HQ visit where they show u and 30 other yo-yo's the only store that's remodeled and isn't comping (MSD%).

potemkin family dollar

(4/5)

do small SM's still do stuff like store visits, definitely, or at least I'd hope so

but the industry is now "the multi-mgr industry."

u cant go off on ur own, any unapproved contact presents risk, and compliance is correct to run it the way they do...the risk/reward is horrible.

reward, u might glean some insight that generates 2 bps of residual

risk, "SAC 2.0" is ur firm's new name

so yea, u aint talking to nobody except these two experts that we approved

maybe go find another way to grind out bps of P&L. or don't, we don't care, we'll have your GMV reallocated to the Industrials team next to you by the time we terminate your email access.

this is how it should be. or at least, this is how anyone with a brain would run a multi-manager fund.

(5/5)

sometimes u hear "no, its all the same info on these expert calls, but u as an analyst can incorporate it differently."

I'm pretty sure that's horseshit

when you hear "I'm at 5% with the street at 2%, based on this expert network call from earlier," you kind of just know the whole model is built on air

also these expert network guys make real good dough, like $1000/hour to sit there and say anything

what incentive does he have to give you information that is remotely useful, knowing that compliance is on the call, and knowing that he'll lose this golden goose if he's anywhere near the grey area

realistically he's probably giving 99% boilerplate commentary and prepared remarks, with the other 1% being bad color if he thinks you're a prick

never let them lose sight of who the expert is. you gotta steer a few guys in the wrong direction, and occasionally detonate an entire pod, just so they know whose boss.

END

this is such a great racket for the experts + for the expert network, and there's no doubt in my mind that this is somehow a pass-through expense which gets comped by the multi-manager's LP's.

just a straight-up boondoggle

the once noble arts of scuttlebutt and shoe leather... replaced by a web of misaligned incentives, box checking and CYA...as the nerfing of primary research continues unabated.

something super impactful I read (lost the source). courtesy thing

in any email you send, to anybody

the goal should be that they open it and within 2-3 sentences know EXACTLY what you’re asking of them

because EVERYONE sends long-ass multi-paragraph emails, trying to be detailed and specific on all the little things, and then clicks send

the reader opens it and just sees a giant wall of text. this creates cognitive load/minor annoyance as they now have to parse thru all ur writing and figure out “what is being asked of me here?”

like I said this is courtesy, not linkedin slop. they may never notice or thank you.  but they will silently appreciate you making their life easier

<a id="t-2056386212831572183"></a>

*Single post · [2056386212831572183](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2056386212831572183)*

the 10 best books/resources to learn fundamental equity valuation and markets

plus some books/authors that didn't make the cut and why they were excluded

--

(1/4) - INTRO

so I have a list in my pinned tweet with a bunch of books/series/intvws

its brief by design (30 ish names) - expectations investing, market wizards, mauboussin, snowball, stuff like that

sometimes I get questions "why isnt xyz on there" (someone just asked about Soros' Alchemy of Finance)

I will give my opinion (and thats all any of this is, opinion) as to why some names are were excluded

keep in mind this is directed at someone with 0-10 years experience. after 10+ then I suppose anything is "in play" (ie Alchemy of Finance), but theres no point in reading titles like that w/o having Buffett/Mauboussin down cold

--

(2/4) - TOP 10 / DO NOT PROCEED UNTIL FINISHING THESE

1. Mauboussin - expectations investing

2. mckinsey on valuation

(dull, but this is as close to The Bible as we've got for fundamental equity valuation. there is stuff in here that will pop up time to time where its not "mission critical" but where someone will look at u like...um really? so u want a passing familiarity at the minimum).

3. Mauboussin (total compendium)

go to his page, michaelmauboussin dot com, then go to Writing. obviously there are REAMS of content here, but its similarly Biblical to McKinsey, except more readable and does a better job linking to how mkts actually trade

I've been trading mkts since 09 and I still go read mauboussin notes all the time and find new insights in them.

4. buffettfaq dot com

also the BRK letters, which can be dull, fall under "constantly," meaning, you can proceed without going top-to-bottom, but keep reading these + Mauboussin in ur spare time

again, as with mauboussin, since 09 I still read BRK letters (on brks website) all the time and find new insights in them.

5. Market Wizards + New Market Wizards + Hedge Fund Market Wizards

idk about any of the series after that...you'll lol when you look at the original "Wizards" (its like Druck and PTJ) relative to the newish ones... I'm not gonna throw shade, u can see for urself.

and the critics are correct to point out that without including audited track records for a 2026 "wizard," the crowd is going to call bullshit. we dont need Bruce Kovner's track. we do need it for Twitter Substack Wizards or Wizards of Seeking Alpha or whatever these new collections are called. 

6. Snowball (on buffett)

7. Poor Charlie's Almanack

8. Spend a lot of time on Graham & Doddsville (columbia b-school newsletter), the ones from 2010-2020 had some REALLY good investors on there with great intvws - lot of the storied tiger cub PMs.

as with market wizards, I havent recognized a G&D interviewee in at least six years...not throwing shade, but like, u dont gotta tell me who Lee Ainslie is, u do have to explain "heres a guy who finished MBA in 2024"

9. Spend a lot of time on VIC going thru old pitches. Sort by all-time, highest ranked, spend years on this.

10. Read Ed Chancellor's books esp Capital Returns + Capital Account

Honorable Mention: Lynch/Fisher/Jesse Livermore. Also 25iq dot com, by Tren Griffin, is an awesome collection of investor wisdom, as is mastersinvest dot com.

--

(3/4) NOT ON THE LIST

1. Damodaran. He's worth reading, but overly academic to me...way too far away from how markets actually trade. His DCFs are sound academically but its always like "TSLA is at $1450, but based on my assumptions, its worth $12."  

Like, brotha, ur DCF is missing something lol. Maybe tsla is worth $1000, or $2000, or even $750, but the lack of understanding the stock's narrative is what overly-academic practitioners miss.

I'd still read what he writes - I put him #1 because I want it to be clear, he is as sharp as it gets on academia/theory -- just pay attn to Mauboussin's writing vs his and you'll see what I mean.

2. Taleb. I am not throwing shade but go back and read the Greeks, there is nothing he has said that they didnt cover already - I'm not blaming him, its just a misconception by the readers.

Like referencing a term "ah yes, thats like Taleb's xyz." No, thats Aristotle, or Juvenal (black swan), or Procrustes (greeks), like, the idea that you have to read these as part of a market education is incorrect.

Taleb is highly intelligent and I'm assuming he acknowledges his sources, and I always appreciate a sharp brain that can link old concepts to new ideas (the stock market). So I'm not attacking him personally. This is strictly a "do equity analysts need to read this book" and the answer is no.

3. Soros

this guy's stuff is unreadable to me. I'm pretty stupid, but I recognize dense writing when I see it. you can be both brilliant and a bad writer at the same time.

I have nothing but respect for him as a money manager, he's one of the GOATs. but to learn markets, I mean, you might get one or two good insights from 300 pages of his writing, its just very obtuse

by way of comparison, buffett is both brilliant and a great writer. a genius takes the complex and ELI5's it. a book that is brilliant yet unintelligible is a bad book.

4. Housel

Okay, so, I worship Housel. He should probably be read. He is the fucking man, I have watched his career since 2013 or so when he had no followers and was writing for Motley Fool. 

My immediate reaction was "this guy is one of the best financial writers I've ever seen," and it has been a pleasure to watch him ascend and then bang out a book that has sold 10 gorillion copies. 

Young guys, pay attention, this is how you launch a career off twitter.

Only reason I didnt include him is that I feel like he aims more towards a casual/hobby investor, not a fundamental equity analyst which is who this list is aimed at. 

There is probably a separate list for Housel's category - "How to think about money and markets in general," aimed more at the man on the street. Someone should do a top 10 for that, mine would just be 1. Housel 2. ask Housel for the rest of the list.

Either way he's one of our generation's premier talents in writing about markets, he absolutely has a gift.

5. Ben Graham/Greenblatt/Klarman

Security Analysis is boring as fuck and its okay to admit it. As for the Greenblatt and Klarman books, they're good, but anything "Value Investing" can be deferred/delayed.

the reason is that value investing has been dead for 15 years or so, I dont know why. it'll come back, it always does, people can dunk on Einhorn all they want but he is correct in that value investing is backed by academia/theory/historical perf as a strategy that works.

practically...theres no point in learning "Net Nets" when none exist anymore (or at lesat right now), its like this strategy got arbed out or something. 

I'm not saying "no," I'm saying "go read BRK/Mauboussin instead" - u dont have infinite time.

"but theres net-nets in all these foreign countries" - yeah, there are. and you'll figure out why as soon as you start fucking around with them. 

markets are far too competitive for u to just say "oh wow a $150M company with $160M in cash, free lunch!"

caveat investor

6. Howard Marks

I like him, but the critics are correct. His books/memos are just platitudes...."You should be optimistic, but exercise caution, and always think about reward relative to risk."  Useful if u have questions like "is it sunny in the summer?"

similar to Greenblatt/Klarman/Soros/Graham, there are absolutely a few good nuggets in a 300-page book, but again, we're speaking in Return on Time terms. one Berkshire letter or mauboussin piece = 99.9% of investing books.

7. all the "History" books

tons of these, Madness of Crowds, Barbarians at the Gate, Michael Lewis, A Random Walk, shit like that

worth reading, yes, but not until you've mastered the fundamental shit. again, the "actionable insight per 300 pages ratio" is just not good.

plus sr's and ur boss will just look at you with disdain if you get too, like, "ah yes this current market backdrop is like the 1613 Dutch Tulip Crisis/South Sea Company!" like bro stfu and build the fucking model we hired you to build lol

8. Macro books

there's some books like Volatility Machine that I loved, but for a fundamental equity analyst, you do not need to spend your precious hours (at least at first) learning this stuff

the plumbing of foreign sovereign debt markets, why argentina keeps defaulting, the tequila crisis/mexican peso de-peg

its really interesting, but just not all that relevant....none of this would ever be in a stock pitch

--

(4/4) - END

I think that's it. If you disagree with the top 10, and think a certain title deserves a spot in there relative to one that I have, I'm all ears...the goal is to get the 10 right.

On the non-top-10, please dont argue, I'm not shitting on those people. Nor is there any point to repyling, "heres ten other books I enjoyed," because thats how a guy ends up with 1250 bookmarked titles and doesnt read any of them.

there are TONS of "marginal" finance books. I've read them, you've read them, we don't need a compendium of those.

The goal is to figure out the absolute best books - the ones where for the rest of ur life, if someone asks "what do I read," you know exactly what to say:

"read these 10 books and do not read anything else until you have mastered these." 

because people will actually read those 10.

--

good luck reading
GB

## 65. 2026-05-19
<a id="t-2056831115907027001"></a>

*Single post · [2056831115907027001](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2056831115907027001)*

on unit growth stories - example frameworks/templates to add to this, ways to think abt modeling

7-8 part tweet

I spend an ungodly amount of time on store builds and the 1000 diff ways to think abt/model productivity, white space, value of store base at maturity discounted back...and so on

I think ppl will see that thread below and say "I need some inputs to get to his output." 

I'll give u some

confidence isn't found in one method. its when u have 50 diff methods that all converge, that all say "the answer is around x." 

this is how each new method increases the aggregate confidence interval.

because, from an analytical rigor standpoint, they SHOULD all align. 

a store does X revenue at Y margin and produces Z profit. 

if those #'s aren't somewhat constant, u have something misplaced or misaligned in ur view of economic reality.

1. INTRO

the incremental store, the value driver -- will LLMs be able to help get that right? 

hell if I know. probably. but typically all of the key drivers in there, the 1-3 that u decide matter, they come from:

I. primary research (#1 by far)

II. stress-testing mgmt's estimates of unit economics from their deck/transcripts...they can be lazy, sloppy, obsolete.

so you're constantly breaking down new qtrly info and collapsing into unit-level EBIT, ROIC, ROIIC and so on.

u will find that mgmt's old deck says "we get 25% cash-on-cash" and new boxes are doing 20%. or labor or store-level cogs/opex has changed in the last few months.

the trajectory of new store economics drives the stock

III. historical results (sometimes u break down and rebuild ur model in a new way that reveals some insight or reflects economic reality better)

2. LLM's and "the incremental store"

there's definitely ways to leverage LLM's to forecast how "the incremental store" in Knoxville will do, the amt of data u have relative to a Google search is unlimited

get the address of the new store and then learn everything about the 5 sq mi around it, traffic, comps, privates, there's so much that could flow into a model.

so I don't know, no one does, but the "100th store slowdown" short framework is reasonable, and its one of many. 

sometimes the puzzle is: can they do 1000, 1500 or 2000 stores in the US? stock could double or get cut in half on that

everything is on "the incremental store," and the economics of that store.

--

3. good habits

brett is the man for 5 things that I have to point out, random analyst musings

a. thinking out loud constantly even if only 50% baked. 

stuff good analysts do which stick out like a sore thumb on twtr, where everyone wants to appear certain and confident. paradoxically, the ppl just throwing shit at the wall w/ no conclusion, are the ones who know their shit

b. willingness to go way out of his element and just figure something out re: guardrails, dimensions, frameworks in modeling

sign of a highly experienced analyst and just comes from looking at 10,000 different companies

prob takes 10+ years. younger analysts get stuck "how do I do this, its not disclosed, etc."

but your job is to figure it out. in that case, the "100-store wall," all the moving parts to get to store 100, and then breaking it all down into model-able variables

plus ones u could potentially diligence (demographics/income etc)

if u said "go model this Mongolian iron ore smelter" or "a chain of japanese jerkoff parlors" or something random, a really good analyst just starts flying around. 

also if that japan chain exists its worth 50x, every single japanese tweet is either about American BBQ or wanking. some MBA grads with search fund $$ need to get over there and run the SBUX/CMG or even WING playbook

c. mountains of variables

the answer to "how do I do this" is to just start hurling paint on the canvas and see what emerges as u do, and iterate

literally everything is work in progress, u dont sit and think, u think via action

so even though brett isnt a resto analyst you see him just slapping all these different variables together, which could be paired and flexed in a million diff permutations

this actually gets much easier once you go out-of-sector and realize all businesses are the same; volumes and price, fixed and variable. you may lose some nuance but can get a functional unit-level model pretty fast 

d. in LLM'ifying all of this

I have no aptitude for this stuff yet but again, brett is just taking #2 and using it for #3.

the work is #2, what drivers/variables to use. translating that to LLM is the less-taxing part mentally.

I don't think ppl understand the % breakdown between modeling and LLM

younger guys might just go direct to LLM "build me a model" then tweak that

but this is wrong, you're not being thoughtful on the inputs, this will fuck you up. everyone says it but I just know ppl won't listen.

brett's stuff looks like its all LLM but its probably 90% model inputs, maybe 95%, with the LLM added on the end.

e. model to LLM ratio

a model is your brain's projector screen for how u think about a business and the drivers/line items to flex

don't outsource that part to an LLM. I guarantee Claude can build 100 "new store" outputs and all will be trash

these LLMs can be so deceptive in confidently presenting you something that looks good and is just completely fucking useless

--

4. nas - "all I need is one mic"  (one store)

in both public or private Consumer & Retail it is crazy how much time, and how many different ways, you can try and answer a very simple question:

"What are the economics of one store?"

first, getting a fucking labor build is outrageously hard 

(this is less for pubco's and more owner-operator, equity stake or anything where u can contribute to how the biz model adapts)

but salary vs commission vs tip, flexing hours and humans and motivation, discounts, bonuses, loyalty programs, what line items (or unit economics) to key the worker on

one store. that's it.

5. modeling simplicity

people probably think a L/S pod model is NASA level complexity

when in reality, at least in Consumer, there's just like five tabs all named "store" "store2" "newstore" "regional"

its all just tons of data and primary research building on itself, trying to get some confidence in very complex questions like

"are EBIT margins at maturity 6%, 7%, or 8%"

that drives everything

100 bps of mature store-level EBIT margin, on a 500 store base with runway to 1500 stores?

u can get down 60% or up 350%

one store

6. subprime human capital

if u cant master one store then the entire business cant scale and isnt worth a shit.

ur investor deck has some ambitious growth plans to justify an exit valuation that makes any Consumer concept attractive

and unless u are proven, ur risk as an operator doesnt justify a HSD% return. u are a high-yield credit aka junk

the S&P yields HSD%, you're not appealing until mid-to-high teens.

tends to be a fxn of scale/unit growth.

7. END/moar variables

anyway... we didnt even finish one store. then u have to worry about new vs old stores, store density, cannibalization, competition, ecom/omni

a unit growth story's economic value tends to boil down to "are the economics of a NEW store higher than the current store base?"  (ROIIC > ROIC)

so however u get there, get there. 

random screenshots below from decks/models/mgmt - I'll do a "unit growth" article at some point bc trying to fit shit into 4 screenshots isnt working.

good luck 🫡

> QT @FundamentEdge: In 2008 when I started as a junior analyst at a Tiger Cub, I did a teach in with John Glass of Morgan Stanley. He showed me a very compelling analysis of casual diners and how there was a high propensity for concepts to come off the rails around 100 boxes. The inference was that, around that scale, management focus was diluted and the concept had picked the low hanging fruit and had to stretch into new & unproven geographies. Quite often, the new geographies wouldn't scale and the stocks felt the dual pressure of an earnings re-base and compression of the growth multiple. 

In looking for restaurant short ideas, this ~100 unit threshold become a consistent & reliable "pattern" that I would apply across concepts. This wasn't a deterministic process, but a reliable filtering mechanism for going deeper and hunting aggressively for signs of pattern alignment (often times, great concepts would grow through this level unimpeded).

But it was a lot of work!! It would take me many weeks to build conviction on one of these ideas.

Fast forward 18 years, and I can put a "pattern" like this into an agentic architecture. And with the right data ingested, I could automate analyses like:
> Scoring of new site selection (automated demographics, MSA & brand awareness analysis)
> New unit AUV vs. existing System AUV ramp
> Cohort productivity tracking
> Subtle languages shifts on expansion pace
> Executive departures (COO or real estate office departures were interesting triggers)

Analysis that I would do before, now at the push of a button: i.e. the ability to scale depth in a way never possible.

The deeper I go into agentic processes, the more I realize that deep sector & vertical processes are where the real value is unlocked. There is ~minimal value in a generic earnings preview skill. But an earnings preview architecture encoded with the set-up & reporting patterns powered by your pattern recognition - now we have something. Not "XYZ managed care company is going to miss Q3 earnings", but a deep articulation of guidance hockey stick dynamics in that space, supported by deep regulatory filings work, management sentiment analysis intra-quarter, all my internal notes, a comprehensive healthcare trend tracker, and your own historical trading history in shorting these sort of set-ups. Again...the sort of work I would do as a managed care analyst, but deeply automated.

What does this all mean? How do I build this out?

Well, that's a work in progress. But as a build consideration, here is what i would recommend:
> Begin to attempt to find the patterns in your investing. Patterns vary *materially* by strategy & by sector. I don't think any vendor or consultant (including me) will be able to give you an off the shelf pattern library: this will be deeply personal to your firm, your process, and your risk & duration envelope
> Above all, view the technology stack as a flexible substrate to express these processes & patterns. A pattern such as above was very difficult to wrap in a chatbot (early finance chatbots like Portrait AI did a very interesting job in doing so and was popular amongst generalists for that reason, but it was a lot of work). My sense is some of the highest ROI motion for investors right now is *not* to get fluent in spinning up sub-agents in Claude Code CLI, but to actually put pen to paper on their process & patterns and let the super app / agentic workspaces catch up (I am running this process in Perplexity Computer and something like this takes about 15 minutes to spin up....THOUGH you need the awareness of the underlying logic of this pattern to tell the agentic what to design...this is where the advantage will sit in my opinion).

## 67. 2026-05-19
<a id="t-2056871137875783768"></a>

*Single post · [2056871137875783768](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2056871137875783768)*

most questions on markets, should probably be answered by this initial question:
 
"what is the duration of your capital?"

if its "I dont know"... thats a problem

1/5

bc people will ask 5 entirely different questions, where the 5 answers cannot be made congruent

like where to put something like sentiment in a model or a dcf

or "the bogey"

or u think adobe is cheap but u are also intrigued by korean plutonium smelters

dog you are all over the place

bo knows. but u aint no bo

2/5

what is your plan, what are you trying to do, what are you good at

let me break it into 3 things that matter
1. capital duration
2. body of work/P&L
3. temperament

if you don't know what you're good at, go look at ur P&L over the last 5-10 years

u may not see what ur good at, but u sure will see what u fucking suck at.

"stop doing what isnt working"

3/5

this ongoing cessation takes years, and really it takes a lifetime

but thru this process of, ahem, "creative destruction" in a P&L context, u should find some area where u aren't completely horrendous

and if u dont, well, skydiving probably isnt for u

but a good way to figure out the duration of your capital...is to know what it isn't.

4/5

go back to ur P&L, what's the longest you've ever held a single position

if its "eight days," u prob don't have buffett's temperament. almost no one does

but all those ideas that are 12-24 months on paper, they probably aren't gonna get there

u can revisit them after u unlock this exclusive downloadable content

called

"long-term capital gains"

now, go look at ur trading P&L, is it good or bad

whatever u see that isnt working, stop doing it

5/5

this should all bring u closer to having some sort of grip on reality

and the truth is that its not binary, u may have a few different areas where ur pattern recognition just sort of works, tape feel, an understanding of what drives price in this corner of the market

but when coming at it this way, u know that its X and Y, and are no longer trying to be 50 different people, asking 50 different questions, spending ur day browsing tweets like "oh wow these all sound good"

END

that has to end

u can put the 3 hour sci-fi podcasts away

it doesnt matter which semis are best positioned for data centers in space. u arent gonna own any of them

follow the trail of ur losses

u arent good at oil, u arent good at software, or at shorting, and so on

all the answers u seek are somewhere in ur temperament and ur P&L

they're not on twitter

"what do you mean? you were writing symphonies when you were 14."

there is a gap between intention and perception

probably 20 times now I have looked at a stock pitch or similar and replied w some version of:

“this looks like an LLM did it. You may have done the entire thing by hand, the long way, whether u did or not is none of my business. But someone u send this to - someone who actually matters - is going to have the same reaction I did. And that ain’t gonna be good for ya. Do with this information what u will.”

ur intentions don’t matter

u have to mentally bridge the gap over to the other person’s perception and say “how will this appear to them when they first see it”

this dynamic will only become more prevalent as robots create all this shit, and I guarantee it is not limited to stock pitches, it goes for ALL work product

“but I had good intentions” isn’t enough….world don’t work like that…go the extra step.

from experience,

DFIU > Heroics 

Short for: "Don't Fuck It Up"

Just show up, keep your mouth shut, and execute

God gave you two ears and one mouth for a reason

You'd think that most people had four mouths and no ears

People don't listen, ever

Everything is sales...but "sales" doesn’t always = running ur mouth

Experience teaches you that "sales" is often just being more likable, humble, respectful, and a better listener than the other guy

Most Heroics attempted, just lead to coming off as arrogant, or saying something that they secretly don't like, or showing that you weren't listening

Or just finding 1,000 innovative new ways to trip over your dick and snatch defeat from the jaws of victory

And most situations you're in -- although you don't realize it at the time -- are situations that are "yours to lose."

She didn't spend hours getting ready, thinking "I hope this guy talks about LLM prompts for 3 hours"

Job intvws don't bring you in and say "we can only hope this guy is a rebarbative dingbat"

Discretion is the better part of valor, and I suspect that most people, being brutally honest with themselves, would agree that their worst enemy has by far been themselves

Like Talleyrand said after he and Napoleon murdered the Duke of Enghien: "C'est pire qu'un crime, c'est une faute."

"It was worse than a crime...it was a blunder."

## 73. 2026-05-27
<a id="t-2059622564809343476"></a>

*Single post · [2059622564809343476](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2059622564809343476)*

2nd most important lesson I learned in my 30's

you'll get a far more accurate view of someone's character, by viewing a human being -- any human being -- simply as a pile of as virtues and vices.

this all ties into critical thinking, objectivity, removing emotion and preconceived biases from how u navigate life.

(1/6) - Feelings > Thoughts

we are hardwired to "feel" about people.

we dont really think, no human has ever been rational.

our mind says "I like that guy" or "I don't like that guy," and then we bucket them as good or bad and make excuses for their behavior

unfortunately, even the people u loathe have some redeeming qualities to them

its like that meme: "when the worst person you know makes a good point"

you want to be able to recognize that.

(2/6) - Objectivity > Subjectivity

everything is about assessing people's virtues and vices, from a purely objective standpoint

excellent Kierkegaard quote that sums this up: "Most men are subjective toward themselves and objective toward all others, frightfully objective sometimes – but the task is precisely to be objective toward oneself and subjective toward all others.”

this is why TDS is so deadly, and as many great investors have commented, its bewildering to watch money managers fall so deeply into it

the entire job is based around removing cognitive, emotional, and mental biases

and thus, by being so open about refusing to acknowledge a single virtue -- when every human who has ever lived has at least one -- the crowd is correct in straightlining this "syndrome" to one's investing ability, or lack thereof.

I have no strong view on the man, but I'll be damned if I ever let a single human occupy even .01% of the mental real estate that he takes up for half this country.

(3/6) - How To Do This, Except 100% Wrong 

Ditto for the stoic guy that everyone is getting on -- the crowd is correct in saying "wtf?" 

You prize yourself on emotional self-discipline and equanimity and a powerful mind that withstands the influence of all passions, and then visibly lose your shit because you don't like the President?

Seneca would call you "woman'ish" and effeminate, you really can't hold yourself up as a champion of some philosophy that you clearly don't even practice.

But I don't want to talk about the President, its just a perfect example of what this tweet is supposed to look like... except the complete opposite.

We want 100% objectivity, 0% subjectivity.

These are examples of 100% subjectivity, 0% objectivity.

All new information is processed through the "orange man bad" filter in a way that seems to remove any possibility of objective, rational analysis. 

Purely subjective, purely based on preconceived notions. No action is good or bad; if he did it, its bad, if not, its good.

So take all of this, and use it as an example of what not to do.

(4/6) - Compiling The Dictionary

The first step to shifting your worldview towards virtues/vices, requires a mental dictionary of what all those are.

The full list, the shopping cart, all the characteristics that could potentially assemble like Megazord to form a human being.

God leaves no one empty-handed, on both ends.

I have never met a human that didn't have several redeeming qualities, whether I wanted to admit it or not.

The crowd is assertive in searching for vices in great men, but fails to assign virtues where due.

Chalk it up to envy and pride and vanity and arrogance, the inherent human desire to say "I'm better than you."

But for your own self-interest, you want to be able to recognize, tag, and absorb these traits as you see them displayed in people...inculcate the virtues you like, while avoiding or mitigating the vices.

(5/6) - Give All Men Their Due

This will make you a much more forgiving human, probably more pleasant to be around, and it allows you to treat every interaction as one that you can learn something from.

You'll either want their virtues, or you'll want to avoid their vices -- there's something for everybody.

And in viewing people through an objective lens, or as close to objective as possible, you'll free yourself from emotion, and you'll stop lazily tagging people as "good" or "bad."

There's never been a good man or a bad man; just ones with more virtues than vices, or more vices than virtues.

Give people their due, and more importantly, become an astute, critical, and articulate evaluator of the traits that comprise a single human's character.

(6/6) - Case Study: Napoleon

Example to drive this home

Napoleon is one of the most-written-about humans ever, with estimates as high as 300,000 books that are about him + his empire.

We have tons of primary material. We probably know as much about him as any human who has ever lived.

So we should probably have a definitive answer to this question:

"Was Napoleon a good dude or a bad dude"

The answer?

There is no answer. 

It depends who you ask, on the biographer's opinion, and on his cherry-picked selection of primary material to prove his point.

We only have opinions.

So if we can't say "good or bad" about Napoleon Bonaparte -- a man we know more about than just about anyone, aside from Jesus Christ -- what makes you think you have any more certainty on some random beta cuck from X dot com?

This is why with Napoleon and any other character, or biographical case study, we just want break him down into virtues and vices.

Mimic the virtues, and avoid the vices.

END: A Final Wrinkle

The final step is one that we can sum up in Napoleon's own words:

"Du sublime au ridicule, il n'y a qu'un pas."

From the sublime to the ridiculous is but a step.

What is the step?

That a man's virtues and vices tend to be inseparable.

They are two sides of the same coin.

You can't just clip the vice and keep the virtue -- it's as if they are tied together at the tail.

"A man's greatest weakness, is often his greatest strength taken too far."

This adds an entirely new level of complexity to the whole framework, but I'd be remiss not to point it out, if only for your own benefit as you try to rationally assess your own strengths and weaknesses.

Nietzsche's framing of willpower is how he ties this all together. All great men, the heroes and the villians, possess an incredible quantum of willpower.

This quantum makes them capable of inflicting great good, or great evil, upon the world.

The way the cookie crumbled, could have gone entirely the other way. But they possessed some trait, both vice and virtue, that allowed them to shape the world as they saw fit. And just because they chose one direction, doesn't mean they weren't capable of the other.

That's the link between vice and virtue, and that's why La Rochefoucauld opened his "Maximes" -- one of the best books ever written on human nature -- with this legendary banger:

"Our virtues are most frequently but vices disguised."

> QT @gregoryblotnick: most important lesson I learned in my 30’s is that you cannot manage other people’s perceptions of u.

I vividly remember being 34-35 and REALLY struggling with this so I'll explain.

(1/7)

people don't think, they feel.

in your mind u assume that everything about u is being weighed rationally and using the power of reason

in reality, there is no objective reality.

we live in stories. 

we have stories of ourselves and who we believe ourselves to be. 

the other people in our midst are comprised of stories that we have subjectively written about them. and you, in turn, are nothing but a story to others. there isn't just one "story" - each story is subjective and unique based on the individual author in question.

(2/7)

to some people, u are viewed thru a favorable lens, a favorable story. everything u say or do is filtered thru this lens.

their view of u is skewed (and incorrect) in that it's too rosy. their perception is too positive.

to others, u are viewed thru a negative lens, a negative story. ur words and actions are painted with this brush on arrival.

their perception is skewed and incorrect, just as the other person's was -- the only difference is the directional bias. one is too positive, one is too negative. 

in both cases, this is confirmation bias at work, and neither individual may even be aware of this information-filtering mechanism as it takes place.

(3/7)

what you need to understand, is that you are powerless to change either of these two perceptions.

"you cannot manage someone else's perception of you."

put another way, what other people think is none of your business.

humans are wildly irrational. if you haven't noticed, people perceive all sorts of slights or insults that were either unintentional, and quite often, never even occured. imaginations run wild and ppl dream up scenarios where someone is their best friend or worst enemy - with zero basis in fact.

(4/7)

you will drive yourself CRAZY trying to empathize "why does this person think what they do?"

because the truth, most of the time, is that they don't even know.

the subconscious is something like 95% of brain activity on the high end, or "the vast majority of it" on the low end. we have a very, very limited understanding of what makes us think, feel, say, and do the things that we do.

at some point, your actions, which are straightforward and logical to you, will be perceived on a wide spectrum by others. this will baffle you - how can all these people draw different conclusions about what I say or do, or even who I am as a human being?

(5/7)

but this is reality.

you are perceived differently, you have a different "story," in the mind of every single person you will ever meet.

is there room for their perception to shift positively or negatively based on your actions - of course.

yet how much power do u have to control their overall perception of you? very, very little...a number, for your own sanity, best rounded in your own head to zero.

(6/7)

the output is "stop caring what other people think." this is not meant in a sociopathic or thoughtless light. it means that u should do what u believe to be right, and recognize that ur "sphere of influence" extends no further than that.

you are powerless to shape how your words or actions are interpreted by the mind of a given recipient.

there is intention, and there is perception.

you can control your intention. you cannot control their perception.

so don't overthink it, and as a broad way of operating, don't bother trying to manage how other people perceive u... because u simply can't.

in the words of Buffett: “I have this complicated procedure I go through every morning, which is to look in the mirror and decide what I’m going to do. And I feel at that point, everybody’s had their say.”

(7/7)

paradoxically, what u will find is that u become a better person once u stop caring what other people think. 

it is very easy to bend over backwards trying to please other people, or at least doing what we perceive shall please them.

in this process, it is very easy to do the wrong thing while believing it to be right; because it wasn't done for you, it was done for the sake of other people.

yet in trying to manage other people's perception of u, u will not only drive yourself crazy, seeing as it doesn't work, but u will likely go astray, go against your own gut instinct, and effectively betray yourself.

by short-circuiting all of this -- simply doing what you know in your heart to be right, giving it no further thought, and controlling only what lays in your control -- u will find yourself achieving better outcomes with less stress.

that's why the lesson is so important.

hth

<a id="t-2059793754551119960"></a>

*Single post · [2059793754551119960](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2059793754551119960)*

reason 100 of 100,000 why its good practice to keep other ppl's names out of ur mouth

even if you are praising them, and even if you are praising them in private

1/4

this was a month ago to a younger guy, throwing out 3-5 guys that I admire and respect and that I think are worth emulating (if you follow me, u follow them...all use their real names)

nothing came of this fwiw, its to illustrate a broader point

but its an old buffett-ism is to "praise specifically, criticize generally"

give names when praising, talk traits when criticizing

2/4

negative traits are a matter of opinion I suppose, but I'm pretty vocal on disliking envy, cowardice, nastiness, snark, all the dunking and cutting ppl down...a lot of stuff that is common on twtr, but in real life will get u dinged in an interview

personality tests were not part of the typical L/S intvw process 10-15 years ago

they are damn near mandatory at multi-mgrs now for any mid/sr position + some juniors

they may even be the most important part of the intvw

the common negative thread I see in ppl, is "non-team-playerness" and passive-aggressive/indirect/toxic communication styles

resentment, envy, these traits undermine a team from inside and are rooted out

3/4

so...after those, I gave three names of ppl on here who I feel embody all the "right" traits

only in hindsight did I reflect and say, that mightve been a bad idea.

which means the buffett-ism is insufficient.

why?

because here's what will inevitably happen at some point.

4/4

reply = "ok so u say those three guys are good....what about this other guy I follow, so-and-so?"

now u are in a really bad spot.

lets say u think the guy is a clown

u cant say that, or ur just a gossip queen and DM shit-talker. 

u also don't want to say "yeah he's good," or else ur steering someone in the wrong direction.

every answer is problematic. 

"no comment," silence, these are all loaded answers

the fact that u arent giving praise, tells the other person how u really feel.

so even in the process of trying to stand on a very, very conservative principle -- "speak only praise" -- u are going to eventually run into problems.

END

thats why u keep mfs names out of ur mouth...just a matter of tact

u are gonna deal with situations like this IRL, or at least, any ppl who deal with ppl on a regular basis, are going to have to

u may as well practice like u play - don't speak on ppl who arent in the room. never ends well, and sends the signal to all present "soon as ur not in the room, I do u the exact same way."

anyway if u have a better principle or framework, or my logic is off somewhere, i'm open to updating this view.

otherwise...the philosophy can be summed up as follows:

🤐

## 78. 2026-05-28
<a id="t-2059943349956579455"></a>

*Single post · [2059943349956579455](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2059943349956579455)*

re: MBA and L/S equity

these questions are super common

both from "should I go" and "I'm graduating soon, what do I do"

I will lay it out as I see it, it because it is largely the same today as it was when I graduated 12 years ago.

--

1. do not go get your MBA thinking you'll graduate into an L/S seat

this is not a thing. the odds are ~0%.

I'm not even sure its a thing at HBS or Stanford (correct me if wrong)...which means it isnt a thing anywhere.

12 years ago, some L/S SM's recruited post-MBA, or would hire MBA summer interns for a full time role

that entire side of the industry is under pressure.

columbia mba used to get decent flow just because its 40 blocks from midtown, recent grads say theres nada.

2. understanding the hierarchy

out of all the potential hires for an L/S fund, an MBA graduate is bottom of the barrel.

>you are "cold" (2 years out of the game)

>you're expensive relative to an undergrad hire and probably not much more qualified

>your competition = L/S guys who just got let go, and are "plug and play"....SS ER, ditto....PE/IB guys who could ramp quickly...LO analysts

there is just very little reason for an L/S firm to hire an MBA graduate....2-3 months to ramp vs. 1 week

so its not that u are stupid, or that u would be a bad analyst, its that the industry is viciously fucking oversupplied.

3. potential paths

just to be clear, if u get into a top MBA program, drop everything u are doing and go

its worth it on 1000 different levels -- its just that "feeder into L/S seat" is not one of them.

if ur in some shitty job, go back, rebrand urself, teach urself skills, and u can pivot into a better job (even if not L/S)

if u are dead-set on L/S, the two paths that seem most sensible to me:

A. post-MBA SS ER program:

this is by far the smoothest fit/plug-and-play into a multi-mgr L/S seat.  its literally the same job, 90% of it at least....coverage model, 50-60 names, trading prints, mgmt access.

a PM could get you ramped/scaled and with $ in ur ideas in like, 2 weeks

SS ER is the same job: "be the smartest guy in the room on ur sector."

if u do one of these programs for 1-2 years, and u are talented, u will not have a hard time transitioning

B. small L/S SM

this is less direct and u will have to hustle to find a seat

work ur ass off for 2 years, become a genuine expert in a sector, and then try and transition from there

again, they wont hire on campus, u will have to bust your fucking ass to land somewhere

but a few years of "real" reps and u should be intvw-ready

--

I really think that's it...all the other paths are less direct (LO/IB/PE etc)

just very very competitive and u are best off thinking in "hops and skips," 1-2 years here, 1-2 years there, and THEN u get to ur destination

while also expecting 6-12 months of hustle to land each skip

the industry is just horribly oversupplied relative to seats + seat growth

open to input/feedback if anything written here is wrong.

## 84. 2026-06-05
<a id="t-2062897936736919607"></a>

*Single post · [2062897936736919607](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2062897936736919607)*

insanely impressed by two things

1. the amount of young analyst talent on here (like ages 18-21) doing models/pitches at a post-MBA level

2. the degree to which these same individuals have locked in summer L/S gigs, or even full-time, at multi-mgr funds

few other thoughts, some I feel like were already written in the article, some are updated beliefs

3. ppl always forget that multis are talent evaluation machines above all else

always have been (ie the story of KG flying to enron)

so the idea that they've extended that to the undergrad level, probably shouldnt be surprising

but for the ppl who hate on the model for whatever reason, or think it isnt sustainable, you gotta ask "which way is the wind blowing"

feels like they're only becoming more and more entrenched as L/S becomes more "institutionalized"...just like there's bulge bracket banks, there's now 4-5 bulge bracket multis

4. all of this is a good thing

the idea that there's a clear path from undergrad to L/S is so awesome to me, like all of this is

the mentorship/training and so on, plus a clear path to rise thru the ranks to sr analyst and PM. all as close to a meritocracy as you can find

this was always a weak point for the industry, lots of hiring done thru recruiters, it only makes sense that the dominant players would say "why dont we do this all in-house"

also all the random collaborative programs where you do 1 year at a bank and then transfer to a multi (like the UBS/Millennium one)

5. shots on goal

even ppl I talk to where they're playing from behind, like bad school or bad grades, are still getting looks

and attitude is everything, attitude/personality tests have become more and more important each year

so sometimes ppl respond to these like "well thats great that the harvard kid got an intvw, what about me"

just keep trying, I think if u talk to anyone that has ever dealt with HR at any of the multis, they'd tell you that these are all really well-run businesses...ur info stays on file, shit changes, maybe 2-3 years go by and ur talent level rises just as something opens up

6. later stages

on the "age 40" ceiling, none of that is specific to this business or even new

think back 30 years if u were trading prop at a bank, like structured products or distressed debt...around age 35-40 its shit or get off the pot

same for tech or PE or anywhere where "partner level" considerations become a thing

in the article I mention that if you DCF'ed L/S as a career, its a horrible choice relative to many others...but if u have passion u dont care

but as far as there being runway to age 40-50 and beyond -- for the best of the best, there always is

"there are no PMs over 50" is silly, if u look back over the last 5-10 years most of those guys tried to spin out, but its an open question (one that an allocator can answer much better than me) -- perhaps they shouldve just stayed put?... the grass aint always greener

what isnt getting mentioned, is that no one leaves a seat like that by choice. if you're 45 and have $10B of GMV and 5-10 analysts and are doing numbers, u dont just say "well that was fun" and leave...ppl have bills to pay (usually outrageous ones), private schools, 2nd homes, club dues.

its just an uncomfortable reality of any highly-competitive/performance industry, that around age 40 you've either made it or you wash out...nothing is new under the sun.

overall

overall the way I said it in the article, is basically that the path to the top for the top 0.1% of talent, has never been more straightforward

if you believe in "may the best man win" then what is there to dislike? nothing.

so for younger ppl who are doing the damn thing, there are plenty of older ppl on twitter happy to help, but the truth is that u are in the drivers seat...u control ur destiny...nothing u read on Twitter will matter as much as DFIU

Don't Fuck It Up 😎

last thing I'd emphasize to someone younger is that there is no “start giving back when ur older"

like, when ur 23, u already want to try and be giving 21 year olds a foot in the door. it starts immediately

a basic understanding of loyalty and gratitude will take you so goddamn far in life it’s amazing

I’ve fucked up so many times but always found that as long as you’ve been consistently putting ur neck out for ppl -- and it REALLY doesn’t take much, just being thoughtful and doing more than 0 (which is what most other ppl are doing) -- like, ur gonna be good.

people overrate reputation and underrate individual relationships. reputation is nebulous. relationships are REAL. relationships are human beings. 

humans are the blasts of light which penetrate the fog, that cut thru any and all bullshit, and provide shelter from the storm. contrast this with many ppl who prioritize reputation > relationships and have 0 real ones when the chips are down -- extremely common

do not expect reciprocity from all ppl, u never know where it’ll come from, and it’ll fail to hold a lot

ppl dont show up, not because theyre bad ppl deep down but because they are grossly self-involved

so just set this on autopilot ("I'm gonna operate this way and expect nothing in exchange") - best way to keep ur head about u and avoid disappointment.

overall if u talk to someone who has fallen on their ass a lot, been fired, crashed out, they'll tell u that the ppl who were there for them...sometimes its "from the goodness of their heart"...far more often its "you were there for me, so now I'm here for you." 

the grand quid pro quo of life.

so I'd sum this all up in this one principle:

"If you want reliable friends, you have to be reliable first."
