## 270. 2026-07-18
<a id="t-2078576792554860973"></a>

*Single post · [2078576792554860973](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2078576792554860973)*

PTJ - on time stops;

"When I trade, I don't just use a price stop, I also use a time stop. If I think a market should break, and it doesn't, I will often get out even if I am not losing any money."   

Very very tool/skill to add to ur repertoire

Because u anticipate, “market will do X over next 2 weeks.”

Forgetting P&L, if the market isn’t acting how u thought it would, why are u still in the trade??? Exit

To do otherwise is to operate w no plan or no discipline

If the tape feels off relative to ur expectation, exit early, exit immediately

Lay the plan out and if the mkt doesn’t conform to the plan, exit asap, draw up new plan

Hanging around in a busted idea bc ur like, lost, or have no other ideas….ur discipline is fucked. It’s awful. U cant even execute on little plans, don’t expect to be trade calmly in a crash tape. 

Lots of trading is making little promises to urself, repeatedly, and being ironclad on every single one

U break one, u are fucking worthless

Ironclad or nothing, no good trader is a “lots of exceptions” thinker

Add time stops, get used to them, both PTJ and Platt support them, and are very good for keeping u honest and adhering to the plan u PREVIOUSLY laid out

Changing plans on the fly….NGMI

Plan it out, full range of outcomes, execute when path arrives, exit/stop out, new plan, repeat

Be a robot. Be professional. Guys like PTJ, the trade entry, take profit, and stop out…lot of times guys just put in all 3 at once. Then u chill

Nothin to think about

Experiment w all this shit but the “fast trigger finger —> revert to neutral/flat/cash”, having that shit lightning quick, is what will ensure u always live to fight another day.

## 273. 2026-07-21
<a id="t-2079531756068184451"></a>

*Single post · [2079531756068184451](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2079531756068184451)*

"TRADE QUIETLY" - on groupthink, copy-trading, pre-mortems, social proof, and borrowed conviction

(1/8)

this is something that affects new/mid-level traders, although the truth is that it affects people all the way at the top when u make ur ideas public

the way I'd frame it is that you want your ideas driven 100% by fundamentals, or making the technically-correct play (ie blackjack), or executing on whatever gameplan you laid out in advance.

thats solely you vs yourself, or you vs the market.

obviously the first "checkpoint" here is, "am I even laying out a gameplan in advance and then executing on it, or am I always just shooting from the hip?"

(2/8)

a gameplan includes a pre-mortem

>what does it look like when I'm wrong?
>where is my stop, where is my exit?
>what are my known unknowns, and unknown unknowns?

that third one to me, is that for any idea, there should be an X that is foreseeable -- "if X happens, I am wrong, and I exit." basically what breaks ur thesis.

additionally, there are also many Y's, unknown unknowns...this is when something comes out of left field, but you recognize it for what it is: "oh shit, my idea is totally fucking wrecked." and you exit.

the 2nd one is where confirmation bias is a killer

(3/8)

this is why I have to at least caution against groupthink/social/community trading

theres always tons of these in crypto, or gme, amc, bored apes, nft's, opendoor, spacemobile, 1000 others like this...I'm not knocking any of these ideas individually, they may work

its more about being brutally honest with urself, because each element of social proof u introduce into a thesis....is taking u further and further away from that 100% pure fundamental/execution standpoint.

groups form groupthink, and groupthink has a nasty way of magnifying confirmation bias

so when X or Y hits -- the risks -- groups tend to coalesce around some way to nullify the truth

"no, this isnt bad" "its actually bullish" etc

(4/8)

this is how you end up with truly massive losses, or ride something to 0

because you have no exit, there is no gameplan, and the market is too powerful for "we all buy together and itll go higher" to be a sustainable investment philosophy

there's some part of human nature where it feels better to ride something to 0, as part of a group, than break off alone and be like "nah this shit is broken, I'm out"

so idk who this is aimed at, I think ppl with 0-2 years experience will be like "lol stfu nerd", its 2-5 who recognize, yeah, I've taken way too many losses doing this

(5/8)

at the highest level, u see this all the time in stuff like VRX, and these are people who genuinely do their homework

but "all the smartest guys I know are also long" has a way of enforcing confirmation bias...making it harder to peel out when u sense something is wrong

and then, right now, lets say you are gigalong semis and very very public about it

tomorrow, lets say out of left field, SemiCo announces "we just brought 50% new supply online overnight...capacity expansion"

this affects the entire industry/pricing superstructure -- revs, GM%, EPS, all those ests have to come down to reflect new supply

(6/8)

so even though your public-gigalong-stance may not be "broken," u could run thru all ur models and suddenly the risk/reward isnt there anymore.

the mkt priced in this new information and now all ur ideas are 50% down, 50% up, totally balanced

meanwhile u are super public on this thesis

very very few people have the brutal self-efficacy, honesty w self, immunity to social proof, sharp intellect, whatever u wanna call it....to just come out the next day like "yea thats a wrap, we exited"

confirmation bias is incredibly powerful, u have to treat all social/non-fundamental tentacles as poisonous, toxic, negatives that take u farther away from pure fundamental execution (which is hard enough on its own).

(7/8)

on borrowed conviction...very old wisdom to never trade on someone else's tips

but twtr/social media have exacerbated this to an entirely new level

here is the issue. someone says they like X, u buy it

but now, every time news comes out and the stock moves 10-20%, u are looking to that person for what to do next

this is not sustainable, at all...bc keep in mind, they're victim to all the biases we just laid out

so they may make the technically-incorrect play, because they've put so many ppl in it, that social proof kicks in.

(8/8)

I have learned this the hard way many times, I used to email out ideas to groups, and only in the rearview can I look back and recognize "I stayed in these positions longer than I should have, neg P&L came from social proof, confirmation bias blinded me to broken thesis" etc

conviction can never be borrowed. if someone else has an idea u like, thats fine, but u have to write ur own gameplan

start to finish...pre mortem, what it looks like when wrong, how u would navigate all the various newsflow items that would come out

u never ever want to be in a position of looking to some other guy for "what do I do now," also because u never learn anything when ur wrong, u just blame them

whereas sourcing ur own ideas, going thru the entire lifecycle of the trade start-to-finish...when it goes wrong, u can reflect and then iterate on where u fucked up.

END

this is a deep and complex topic but I think I've hit most of the basics...munger's "psychology of human misjudgment" is all about stuff like this

I pulled this below, hit LLM's with this stuff until u understand it

ultimately u have to do ur own due diligence always, think for urself, conviction can never be borrowed

dont share ur ideas, dont talk ideas, dont use other people's ideas...only takes u farther from reality.

two words:

"TRADE QUIETLY"

---

old quote but "people overestimate what they can do in 1 year, and underestimate what they can do in 10" is sage advice

I would even flex it to, ppl overestimate what they can do in 2 years, underestimate in 5

just having watched many talented investors/mgrs go off on their own, and that 24-month window ends up wreaking havoc on their process

theres some universal law of chance, chaos and error, where by trying to force ur desired outcome into a 1-2 year window, u are guaranteed to fail...n = too low a number for process to show up in outcome

whereas I think 5 yrs or higher, u are just in the correct frame of mind

its long enough, paradoxically, where u recognize u can only control the 24 hours ahead of u

puts u in "brick by brick" mode...wake up, chop wood, 100% process, trusting that the outcomes will follow

years pass, and u wake up with a fat pile of W's

hard hat aura 🪖

*2026-07-27 · [2081693999312646594](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2081693999312646594)*

Q: How much time do you spend reading in a typical day?

Munger: Oh, it’s huge. I read myself to sleep every night. I read enormously. I like doing it. Not only that, what I found very early in life was that once I learned to read and handle elementary math, I really didn’t need professors or anything. I could figure out almost anything I wanted better from the written material than from having some professor tell it to me, because he’d be going too fast or too slow or telling me something I already knew or didn’t want to know. And so of course I like doing it by reading. 

You look at [Andrew] Carnegie and [Benjamin] Franklin, they had a few years of primary school, they learned everything by themselves by reading. Whatever they needed, they just learned. It’s not that hard. Imagine educating yourself by firelight, no lamps, no electricity, after a day’s brutal work. Our ancestors had it tough.

## 285. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2081902306195378516"></a>

*Thread · 4 posts · conversation `2081902306195378516`*

### 1/4

*2026-07-28 · [2081902306195378516](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2081902306195378516)*

Basic 2-page stock memo template for ppl to use -- this is version 3.0 or 4.0 since the first one in Summer 2025

Note: this is 100% art, no science. My opinion is just one of many. I will link to the Paragon short pitch though below as I think it's a good "aspirational" 2 pager.

(1/3)

Here is what I think is a clean format.

PAGE ONE:
 
--summary dash (takes up first 50% of page.
--investment thesis in 3-5 bulletpoints
--event path, exit px target, valuation, r/r, base/bull/bear.

You can see below that fit should fit CLEANLY and not look cramped. Majority numbers. Very little words.

To me thats a great flow for page one. All the #'s are in the dash, then "here's the setup" and here's the path to getting paid over next 1-8 quarters.

Don't copy my text too precisely, I bet there's typos, its gobbledygook - its just there as a very loose guide for what you want to be talking about.

So right there, PAGE ONE, that's like 90% of a pitch. Before the mf turns to page two, he's gonna be interested, or he's gonna say "this shit sucks."

It should make clear: Here's the stock today, based on P/E * EPS. Here's where I see EPS for FY2, and here's where I see P/E going as my above FY2 EPS becomes consensus. Price target upside 30% base, then some quick bull/bear flexing for r/r.

PAGE TWO:

I left this wide open for the most part. Chances are, you wrote 10-15 pages on this shit. This page two should be where you take the BEST OF THE BEST, from those 15 pages, and cram it into one page.

I gave ideas, but there is no right or wrong.

Remember: In the Paragon pitch, there is no headers, nothing. He just came with 4 paragraphs of text on page 1, 4 paragraphs of text on page two, and bang, that shit was a wrap.

So, I'm posting this because people send me a lot of really badly formatted pitches, just 8 pages of straight text. 

I am a nice guy, generally. But I have met 200-300 "Dickhead PM's." I am fluent in Dickhead PM voice. I know exactly where in your pitch the DPM will say "LOL this is trash, get the fuck out of my office." 

So your pages 1-2 need to be absolute lightning. That's what I'm trying to emphasize. Pages 3 to 30 will be in the shredder if pages 1-2 suck.

But some mix of these (esp page 2), should be enough to make the pitch. Remember, a pitch is a 90-120 second exercise. If you can't get it done in that time, if they aren't interetsed in 90 secs, your pitch is trash or its too verbose. So keep shrinking it.

I will post links to Paragon below just so you can see that "2 pages is too small wahhh" - no, its not, that motherfucker did in 2 pages what wouldve taken you 20. So its possible.

Going forward, I am just going to reply to most DM's with this post, and say "dude, you gotta condense it more -- too many words -- not enough numbers." Because the Dickhead PM is not gonna make it to page 3 if pages 1-2 are trash.

If I sound like a hardass, well, its better that you get dunked on by some random internet loser, than face-to-face for a pod seat that would change your life.

HTH, and again, if anyone else (professional) has a better format, please post your 2-pagers directly below this. I am not an authroity and I am constantly tweaking these formats because I've found them using them in private/SMB/operating roles as well. So its a lifelong exercise.

END

Good luck pitching,
GB

### 2/4

*2026-07-28 · [2081906692875268173](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2081906692875268173)*

Oh, and if there is a single LLM-generated word in the pitch, its a wrap. I won't dick on the guy too hard but I got a pitch earlier about how "street ests have become sanguine after a parsimonious read-thru"...I was like bro this shit is going DIRECTLY in the trash. Parsimonious? LLM's use words no one would ever use.

With every pitch, start from a blank page and write it all, word by word. No LLM formatting or anything. Because 1 word out of place = that guy below. You know who that is? That's shredder. The man is a walking graveyard of parsimonious read-thru's.

And don't use the Claude format either (see second screenshot) - they all look like that.

You see the paragon pitch? That shit was written 100% in Notepad. No charts no nothing. And it hit like a Spartan Laser from Halo 2.  So keep it simple.

### 3/4

*2026-07-28 · [2081912448391528465](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2081912448391528465)*

two more to study

"whats the format?"

fuck the format, these were grand slam pitches. study what matters in there

https://t.co/3U0YwkYKOd

> QT @gregoryblotnick: 2 more excellent stock pitches - SIMPLICITY.

Both on AMZN in 2010-2016 era

One from Dan Sundheim at Viking, another from Tarasoff, we'll do both.

Both were absolute home runs

1. Sundheim - someone emailed me Dan Sundheim's AMZN long pitch when he was at Viking, or I guess wrote down what he said from an idea dinner:

This was 9 bullets and was a career-making trade.

1. Says AMZN best idea in firm – 3rd biggest name in firm at $1.2B, behind GOOG (own $1.6B) and Avago (also $1.2B)

2. Earnings power misunderstood. Higher rev than expected but margin expansion will be way higher.

3. 50M prime members in next year.

4. Retail growth of 20% is sustainable.

5. AWS is 7x larger than competitors. GE migrating to AWS. Seeing major cost reductions.

6. Will be 22% of rev by 2020. Street missing upside. Will not decelerate that much.

7. Margins - retail business moving to 3P model.  Takes no inventory risk. 3P continúes to increase. Growing to 56% of units by 2020. As 3P grows. Retail margins will grow towards peers. Adds 150 bps per year.

8. Overall will get 350 bps of Gm per year. Getting to 50% by 2020. Next investment cycle will be on top of significantly higher GP base. 150B [GP?].

9. Gets to 16.5% EBIT Margin in 2020. $68 in EPS in 2020.  Says it should be +100% in 3-4 years.

--

That was the pitch. Some mix of AWS upside and 3P/1P GM shift that wasnt in street #'s. That was the 1-2 KPI's that moved the needle vs consensus and the pitch worked, for the right reasons. 

I had the trade on because of what he wrote. The street was at 35% GM%'s 2-3 years out and it was VERY easy to get to 40% based on 3P mix shift, then AWS was accretive to operating margins. You had SO much room to be wrong it wasnt funny. But it worked exactly as planned and the stock went bonkers.

2. Tarasoff Amazon pitch

Similar tenets for most of it. Slide 3 was the banger where I saw it (GAAP EBIT margins) and said, this needs to be a 40% position.

Next, he compares AMZN to WMT/TGT and gives thoughts on what run-rate EBIT margins should be. All clicks. Wasn't in street #'s. 

Entire pitch is here: 

https://t.co/UFpmGhPlT0

There's 4-8 other slides in there worth studying, you'll see them.

These are really, really good pitches and if you had conviction, set you up for a 5-10 bagger, EASILY.

Pitches don't have to be complex. Study those two.

Btw here is the AMZN stock chart in 2015 when they broke out AWS

January 2015: $15 stock. 20% EPS gap
April 2015: $20 stock. 15% EPS gap
July 2015: $25 stock, 35% EPS gap.

Then ripped another 30% into year end.

So you had a stock up 150% in 2015

Then in 1Q16 it drew down 40%...followed by ripping almost 250% in a straight line into 1Q18 volpocalypse

This is why I say you gotta go back and look at the charts as they were at the time

You needed iron fucking balls to hold a position like this IN SIZE, not a 5% position, a 20-30% position, which becomes a 50-60% position, and then you go into a 40% drawdown at that size

2nd chart is what it looked like near the start of 2016

Maybe you "got cute" and dodged the 40% drawdown

Nice work

then it ripped 250%, drew down, and then doubled during Covid because why not

"riding multi year winners in size" is easier said than done

Keep in mind Buffett had a 40% AAPL position this whole time (3rd chart) - $25 stock to $180...sized at 30-40%, over $100B in unrealized P&L. 

Oh, he's 90

"Go back to scraping 250 bps of residual P&L on investor days you brokeboi edgework bp-harvesting peasants. Was my $100B of unrealized P&L a factor bet? Hey Charlie, what do you think?"

"Good question Warren. Is my 35% position in Costco a factor bet? What about your GS prefs, you might be long the 'Extortion Factor!"

(both break down laughing)..."tick tick tick! I can't wait to dilute Lloyd Blankfein's equity to zero and send him a box of H&H Bagels, with a letter about how he's overexposed to the Bagel factor. I'd rather own Nebraska Furniture Mart!"

Anyway if you want to make big $, Buffett shows you how

30-40% position, 3-5 year hold

But those charts below are your enemy. Those are what shake you out...

## 288. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2081939163851423983"></a>

*Single post · [2081939163851423983](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2081939163851423983)*

Here's another great Value Investors Club pitch

August 2016: Long Amazon
(link: https://t.co/GZgumJJBfS)

It's not 10/10 like Paragon, but its interesting to see how an idea goes from contrarian to consensus (and how, just like twtr, you have the clowns mocking and dunking - bullish indicator)

Long amzn was NOT well-received at the time. Go through the comments and you can see how relentlessly it was mocked (because valuation sucks, etc)

Amazon was not a consensus long until 2020-2020

It was a turd from 2010-2015

It was only 2015-2020 when the stock went completely bonkers (before anyone owned it), before the narrative was clear. Once "Retail + AWS + Advertising" became the three-legged stool in 2020, consensus long, easy thesis, cheap valuation...the stock has been a total dog since.

In 2016, this is how it was met - check the 4 screenshots.

Everyone agreed it was CONSENSUS

That shit doesn't matter. Look at how Druck talked about his NVDA trade

All that matters is what inning the trade is in

Here's another (2011 Long Amazon - https://t.co/To0wEM4Qtt)

2nd photo shows bull case when this was HUGELY contrarian (before Fire phone flop)

3rd photo "valuation, valuation, valuation!!"

4th - great pickup on NWC dynamics - amzn didnt require incremental capital to grow.

This is why VIC can be very useful for learning new ideas/case studies -- takes a lot of time but worth it.

## 289. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2081948623873446359"></a>

*Single post · [2081948623873446359](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2081948623873446359)*

very solid: "How to Pitch a Stock to a PM, by Bill Miller (Legg Mason)"

(1/2) - INTRO: YOU HAVE 90 SECONDS.

"I want to emphasize in your presentations that if you go into capital markets, you will realize that portfolio managers have ultra-short attention spans.And there is basically no successful portfolio manager of my acquaintance who has ever wanted to hear a story longer than ninety (90) seconds."

"Peter Lynch, when a senior analyst came into a pitch him a stock, would turn on an egg timer for 90 seconds. The analyst had to complete the presentation within 90 seconds and be out of there."

"If you can’t get the portfolio manager’s attention within that time with a convincing case then they will assume that you either don’t have a convincing case or you are not able to articulate it, and you should go back and figure it out."

(2/2) - Stories not Atoms

The world is made of stories, not of atoms. Most people think of the world as analyzing atoms and its constituent parts, and then I am going to figure out how to value it and then describe it. The alternative way to think about it is to construct a convincing story.

Take all your material together and construct a convincing story. If you speak to a portfolio manager, the best thing to say is, “I want to talk to you about Homestore (HOM). It is at $2.25. The 52-week range is $4 to $2 and the all time high was $100 in 2000. 

I think it is a buy for the following five reasons:

1. Bam
2. Bam
3. Bam
4. Bam
5. Bam

The stock is trading a $2 and change. I think it is worth $6 or $8 or whatever. Here is why I think it is worth that. Here are the risks. 

Then you are done. 

In the presentations: no more than five positive points and three negative points. What is it worth and then you are done."

<a id="t-2082040762263613443"></a>

*Single post · [2082040762263613443](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082040762263613443)*

if there's one habit that will help you get more out of twtr + improve your "return on time," it's sizepoasting

>never talk about % moves without also talking position size (given that P&L = gain * size)
>avoid talking tickers without $$ behind them
>avoid "stock talk" with no cost basis attached

P&L talk only

(cost basis + size + positions u are actually in)

if it isn't about that, it isn't about nothing

suddenly, 99% of twtr reveals itself as a waste of time, pointless, empty-headed banter

find other people who roll the way you do, P&L focused, make one another better, iron sharpens iron

markets are about P&L and P&L only

if it's not about P&L then log off, call your mom, do volunteer work, do anything besides tweet all day about about non-P&L things

otherwise you'll end up arguing for 7 hours about sandisk, when he owns $0 and u have a 0.5% position size

or debating geopolitics or data centers in space or laughing at some guy whose AUM went from $25B to $23B

focus on sizepoasting

size makes any idea into a great idea

you can short PLTR with a 3% position and watch it go nowhere, or make RTX a 30% long position and watch it print ATH's every day

run screens and see what's working, there's 5 different sectors printing ATH's and I still only get pitches on "long salesforce"

go spend 3 days on CSX, its a railroad, it prints ATH's daily, and it will be 10000% easier to understand than some dumb ass SaaSdonut

avoid the peanut gallery and paper trading and composite indexes and guys "victory lapping" some 0.75% VXX call position, its all gigantic jerkoff

in the real world u have to execute. turning a good idea into green P&L is really fucking hard. when u tweet u dont have to execute

so do u want green P&L or do u want tweets

find stocks with fundamentals inflecting higher/accelerating growth, plus technical strength (breakout to 52w/ATHs), find one that you feel you can master, and do what fucking Buffett and Munger do

make it a 30-40% position and sit on it for 3-5 years

"do you want to make money or do you want to 'be right' by sending snarky tweets all day?"

weird-ass dork behavior

post size post P&L post cost basis, find others doing the same, ignore all this other shit

tepper, druck, soros, all of them telling u how the game works

be patient + stfu + expect that u will get 20 "elephants" in ur life + when that elephant finally shows up...u blow that motherfucker's head off

everything else is noise

le monde est a vous 😎

> QT @gregoryblotnick: Position Sizing, "Mental Capital" and Cognitive Dissonance

## 293. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082050170036854836"></a>

*Single post · [2082050170036854836](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082050170036854836)*

"can a stock pitch be only text"
"can it be 2 pages, 3 pages"

look, this Paragon short pitch is literally two pages (screenshots)

https://t.co/ys1OpCPKea

its all text

its eight paragraphs

is it okay if its 3 pages instead of 2, yes

what matters is that there's a logical flow to it all

I ran thru most of it in the QT but read how this one is written

heres the co, the setup, cap structure, here's EBITDA, heres fundamentals/industry fundamentals, here's where EBITDA goes next few years, here's what the street thinks and heres why they're wrong, here's waht mgmt thinks and here's why they're wrong, here's why there's no equity value, here's why you can even short the fucking bonds, and one by one, here is me demolishing every risk that could get in the way of this happening.

boom.

you dont need all the fancy headers I laid out if u know what ur doing, which this guy does

at first though, those are there to help u organize ur thoughts

dont lose sight of what this is about, ANY memo, ANY business writing

after 2 pages u want the other person to say "holy fuck this is amazing"

if it gets you to that point then its right

> QT @gregoryblotnick: paragon (cont)

re: random DM q's, just highlighting more shit the pitch does well, starting at top

intro: format

esp "what abt format"

there is no format, anything goes really

fair question if ur young... u want it to look good

but lot of times, its just u and 1-5 people talking, for hours, the "format" isnt a thing except in an intvw

a lot of these VIC pitches are straight text and they're effective

generally though, I think a good north star is: a good pitch should be a LEAN 2 pages and every paragraph punches them in the face - put all the fluffy shit, tables, graphs after that

if u can write 2 pages of straight lasers like this, u can write text

but for 99% of analysts, this would've taken them 10-20 pages to get their point across (and not been nearly as good)

the only way you can write a "text model" like this is from having spent 500 hours staring at Excel, he clearly knew Noble, knew the assets, had familiarty with all this

note how there's no fat, there's no two-paragraph business description

not even "paragon sells rigs"

in one sentence u get the entire setup and it works

if u can write one sentence that good, then do it

u don't need all the formal "variant view" etc,

he does none of that, but its all included in there

best way to frame it is prob, "I have 7-8 paragraphs to make my point, what do I want to talk about in each one, how do I write this shit so hard and tight that by page 1.5 he's buying puts on his phone"

1. maint/growth capex, there's just no other way to have that level of conviction than 5-10+ years doing primary research on the subsector.

and not just developing variant view on maint/growth split, but "here's why mgmt's guide is wrong"

not important where he worked at the time other than, top-tier SM, europe based -- but thats rsch driven by both corp access + primary rsch

so if ur 20 years old u are not gonna write a pitch like this

tricky thing w/ maint/growth capex is u never know when you'll get paid, or if its priced...it affects FCF, but there's no catalyst that shifts mkt perception

esp bc maint capex isnt always disclosed

and then the pitch relies heavily on mgmt being idiots, which can go other way esp on short pitches (very easy to get hyperbolic), but I have no criticism here

but I wouldn't put like "they sounded dumb last transcript" as core of the pitch...its pretty clear that here, he just knows the whole space and how paragon sounds relative to rest (overly optimistic, poor operators)

so on maint/growth/fcf, how to think abt it re: event path, I don't know

but a pitch that goes right into fcf is never wrong, the guy that is fucking around with ebitda multiples is the one who is wrong

this is a far superior analyst to me, and prob 99% of people I've ever met, I blindly defer to him

you know what an ebitda based pitch doesn't take into account?

maint/growth capex

some dumbass prob was flexing 3x, 4x, 5x ebitda on this enterprise (bull or bear)

zero variant view

thats "average" analyst, this is a great analyst

2. all throughout the pitch just running down the ebitda to fcf math as variables

ebitda less capex less cash int less cash taxes gets u fcf

can default to that always, it works, its fundamentally sound, and u avoid messy/immaterial CFO stuff

cash int and cash tax wont change, u are just flexing ebitda and capex

and so as u analyze or adjust line items (revs, cogs, opex) ur right there

3. the rev components, most will go over the head of anyone who doesnt cover the space, but u can see rev = dayrates * utilization

its hard from the outside to see what drives his assumptions but idk if it matters

bc all throughout, he comes at ebitda from several angles.

the model might start at ebitda, a lot of models do (built out by segments, segment ebitdas all flow to main tab ebitda, and u flow down to EPS from there + use the same capex/cash int/cash tax as before, bang, FCF

when you know a company cold, that's what a 10+ year exp L/S analyst buyside model looks like

there's 3-4 variables that matter (as in this pitch) - the BS/CF, whatever

here, it prob goes to ebitda and then the rev math is a sanity check, with individual rigs broken out as a 2nd sanity check above revs

i've seen these models, they are a mess, u model all these individual rigs

but trying to flow 40 rigs into total rev is silly -- and all the warm/cold stacking, u model that, its GIGO

so something like this, esp where u are playing for a terminal outcome...

"better vaguely right than precisely wrong"

the way hes does it is great in the sense of, we have certainty in capex int and taxes to the point where ebitda being 400, 500, 600 or 800...u have a lot of room to be wrong

just constantly running bull/base/bear on everything as u go through it, and u dont even need to be laying it out fancy model style

here's base, if we sensitize x y we get z, other way we get zz

that's how you turn 1 page of text into 1 paragraph

10 page pitch should be 10 paragraphs, cut two, you have a lean 2 pager

4.  fcf

the fcf/dcf math is simple here and unfortunately not every stock is so easy to run in ur head

in any case here u only have a few years fcf remaining so u can sensitize it the way he does

i'm not sure i've seen a pitch on a stable company that could do a "paper dcf" I guess you could call it...if fcfs are predictable then you probably could

but the pitch is fundamentally sound...no P/E * EPS, like, these are the fcfs

mkts price off fcf, not eps

there's asset value for co's like these, he deads that angle and keeps going...the pitch pre-empts all the questions that a PM would ask

"could they do xyz"

a good short pitch has all the bull tenets available (from street) so if you can just take a machete and slice shit to pieces for 8 paragraphs, thats gonna be a good pitch

5. capital structure

almost absurd to read

this is one of those pitches where you are so far away from consensus, and from market pricing, that you have to wonder wtf you're missing

sitting there like "why isnt the market cap 0 right now, what could I be missing"

I'll tell you what you're missing

its called conviction, and an appetite for risk

they go hand in hand

but this is just a high-conviction pitch, like everything is sealed the fuck up

there are so few analysts who even have the balls to write a pitch like this, much less get it right

"the equity is a zero and the debt is a good short" is an outrageous bar

like u better nail this fucking call, because u are straddling the line on cocky vs confident

pitching a fucking equity short with such conviction that at the end, u sprinkle in that this equity is so fucked u can short the bonds

bygone era, u cant do that at a multi

and this pitch wouldnt exist at a multi...prob too illiquid I'm guessing

and you'd have to own something against this squiggly little levered equity turd

btw, if u look at the stock chart, I think this IPO'ed at $18 and went to $9

so imagine the chart

down 50% in a straight line

u walk in with ur pitch "short it down 50% because its going to zero, press it all the way down and also short the fucking debt"

if u are a L/S analyst, ur gut instinct shivered right there at the thought of shorting something after its off 50% in a straight line

too many scars from shorting something down 40% into a print and it goes up 50% on a miss

much less some shitco like this where the fundamentals are bad, but if the backdrop changes, this shitco equity stub will rip your fucking dick off

you see these 2-3x off the lows on just a small move in oil, crowded positioning, no real change in fundamentals

but thats it bro, I don't remember seeing any other VIC pitches that were as sharply written. everything about this would be fine as a multi-mgr pitch

most VIC pitches are snoozers, like NVR homebuilders

"we see book value compounding at 23% for the next 5 years"

useless in multi mgr L/S

end

somehow I forgot

the very first line being "price target = $0"

hero shit

courage and conviction are what separate an elite analyst like this from the rest, every PM will smile on these traits bc no one has them

u think some kid that just came from KKR to Millennium is gonna slam his dick on the table with a pitch like this

he doesnt know what "bid-ask" is yet

think thats it...if I missed anything DM.

## 295. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082056531257029056"></a>

*Single post · [2082056531257029056](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082056531257029056)*

just one man’s useless opinion, but if you’re just now starting ur career (say 18-25 yrs old)

use your real name on here + photo + lnkd, whatever you’ve got, and just be yourself, be who you really are, talk the way you would irl

why do I say this? because I get CV’s from ppl who are anons, and then the CV is like:

>"college golfer"
>"D1 baseball"
>"fraternity president"
>"college tennis"
>"high school captain/QB"
>"ex-military"

all at great schools, ivies, UVA, UMuch, shit like that

even gutter ivies like the patriot league or the NESCAC send a positive message like "multidisciplinary drug addict" (3-4 substances). Munger spoke of this

but like, bro, by being anonymous, u are literally giving up ur greatest advantage

"college athlete" opens more doors than any of ur dumb ass stock pitches

(this is specific to finance/law/tech etc, NYC rules, SF does shit different)

but at 20-21 people are evaluating you entirely on attitude, a pitch will be like "yeah sure okay" and they know the CV is mostly bullshit

but athletics or military speak volumes, and/or fraternities depending on the school

People love hiring athletes/military, tends to indicate above-average team playerness and discipline...in multi-mgr L/S right now, these are HUGE

personality tests weren't a thing when I was younger

but now, they can make or break your entire intvw process, and they should -- you see on the other side how important it is to have ppl that u want to be in the trenches with

and how bad a toxic motherfucker can ruin a team

so, overall, the alternative path here makes zero sense to me: what is gained by using a fake name?

I cannot name a single upside, every answer can be countered with "you're doing shit you shouldn't be doing in the first place"

specific to finance which is a very name-heavy slash prestige-whore industry, your name/CV say everything

"yale/gs/kkr/hbs"

thats who you are basically

but having a fake name only breeds bad habits, and every single one can be countered with "you shouldnt be doing that in the first place."

a lot of guys in their 40's-50's who have an alias are stuck, for 100 reasons...made too many enemies, LARPing, dirty laundry, etc

but when ur 20-25 and still building habits and character, being held accountable for everything u say is the road u want to walk...coming in at the right altitude as exactly who u are

go read biographies, read "the eminent dead," ask how they'd handle this. would Teddy Roosevelt talk shit from a fake name or just walk into the bar and smash a chair over your head...no brainer

many young ppl have been receptive to this when I tell them, so I'm comfortable tweeting it

if u have big dreams, lotta ambition, and want to be #1 at whatever u do...people are gonna know ur name buddy

the crowd is gonna talk about u and u are gonna have to get comfy hearing what they have to say

so u may as well get used to it now

end rant / if u disagree don't @ me

## 296. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082063764740870191"></a>

*Single post · [2082063764740870191](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082063764740870191)*

I might have been lucky rather than good, but this single slide from Tarasoff's AMZN deck in 2012, was the core of an entire long pitch

Because you could look at street ests for GAAP EBIT margins 3-5 years out, they were in the 0-4% range

Then, you go back and look at that table and see that in 2004, AMZN had a GAAP EBIT margin of 6.4%

And you're sitting there, like, this has got to be the most obvious pitch I've ever seen, or I'm missing something

"Is there a reason why 6.4% is no longer achievable?"

But if you understood the rev model and fixed cost structure, which prob takes 5-7 years of just looking at businesses...you're like...bro this business is a fucking gigantic warehouse selling shit over the Internet.

There is MASSIVE operating leverage. If revenue was $1B in 2004, and it's $10B in 2014, like, those margins are going to rip. I don't even need to be precise -- if it's above 6.4%, and the street is at 4%, I'm getting paid.

This is actually the type of shit the street will mismodel, and all you had to do was go look at 10-20 years of financials

Obviously you want to spend 2-4 weeks testing EVERY line item in cogs/opex, like, is one of these delevering? Are shipping costs problematic, like, what part of their cost structure is not going to lever on DD%+ rev growth?

And eventually, especially once you layered in the GM% accretive 3P/1P mix shift, you're just like...this is a home run man, the bears think this business is a 0% EBIT margin business and they are about to get their dicks blown off.

In 2014-2015 you had AWS kick in, but this was going to work regardless. You had private label. Then you had advertising. You had all these margin-accretive revenue streams flowing in, and street ests were embarrassingly low.

The bull pitch was making its way around (both from sundheim and tarasoff and many others) but no analyst could get their PM to buy the stock, because the valuation didn't make sense

EBIT margins were 1.8% -- this was trading at 250x EPS and like 100x EBITDA and shit

I vividly remember being like, "on a 6.4% margin, this is cheap as fuck."

"When will they get to 6.4%?" 

"I don't know."

Pitch dead. That's how this went. No one owned it in size until the valuation was "safe" 5 years later, and then all the stock has done is u/p.

So idk what lesson to draw from this one - I think TSLA bulls prob had a similar framework. But between all the tailwinds you had, AND you had bezos who was a ROIC/FCF guy, everything aligned for a massive jockey bet.

If you have an alternate take here I'm all ears, otherwise, "that's a 20 hole punchcard...19 to go" is my takeaway.

> QT @gregoryblotnick: 2 more excellent stock pitches - SIMPLICITY.

Both on AMZN in 2010-2016 era

One from Dan Sundheim at Viking, another from Tarasoff, we'll do both.

Both were absolute home runs

1. Sundheim - someone emailed me Dan Sundheim's AMZN long pitch when he was at Viking, or I guess wrote down what he said from an idea dinner:

This was 9 bullets and was a career-making trade.

1. Says AMZN best idea in firm – 3rd biggest name in firm at $1.2B, behind GOOG (own $1.6B) and Avago (also $1.2B)

2. Earnings power misunderstood. Higher rev than expected but margin expansion will be way higher.

3. 50M prime members in next year.

4. Retail growth of 20% is sustainable.

5. AWS is 7x larger than competitors. GE migrating to AWS. Seeing major cost reductions.

6. Will be 22% of rev by 2020. Street missing upside. Will not decelerate that much.

7. Margins - retail business moving to 3P model.  Takes no inventory risk. 3P continúes to increase. Growing to 56% of units by 2020. As 3P grows. Retail margins will grow towards peers. Adds 150 bps per year.

8. Overall will get 350 bps of Gm per year. Getting to 50% by 2020. Next investment cycle will be on top of significantly higher GP base. 150B [GP?].

9. Gets to 16.5% EBIT Margin in 2020. $68 in EPS in 2020.  Says it should be +100% in 3-4 years.

--

That was the pitch. Some mix of AWS upside and 3P/1P GM shift that wasnt in street #'s. That was the 1-2 KPI's that moved the needle vs consensus and the pitch worked, for the right reasons. 

I had the trade on because of what he wrote. The street was at 35% GM%'s 2-3 years out and it was VERY easy to get to 40% based on 3P mix shift, then AWS was accretive to operating margins. You had SO much room to be wrong it wasnt funny. But it worked exactly as planned and the stock went bonkers.

2. Tarasoff Amazon pitch

Similar tenets for most of it. Slide 3 was the banger where I saw it (GAAP EBIT margins) and said, this needs to be a 40% position.

Next, he compares AMZN to WMT/TGT and gives thoughts on what run-rate EBIT margins should be. All clicks. Wasn't in street #'s. 

Entire pitch is here: 

https://t.co/UFpmGhPlT0

There's 4-8 other slides in there worth studying, you'll see them.

These are really, really good pitches and if you had conviction, set you up for a 5-10 bagger, EASILY.

Pitches don't have to be complex. Study those two.

on BRK's Washington Post investment; Munger has called it both a 50-bagger and a 100-bagger ("basis $10M, became worth $1B") but I can't find any support for either

1. Munger: "The Washington Post was just a trove of intrinsic value. Here was a time of low stock prices and panic, and a place where one stock got clobbered for an unusual reason to a suddenly low level. We just charged in and bought $10 million worth of The Washington Post, and in due course it became worth $1 billion."

2. Munger: If you look at Berkshire's investments where a lot of the money's been made and you look for the models, you can see that we twice bought into two-newspaper towns which have since become one-newspaper towns. So we made a bet to some extent.... The Washington Post was a rare opportunity indeed. Munger: In one of those - The Washington Post - we bought it at about 20% of the value to a private owner. So we bought it on a Ben Graham-style basis - at one-fifth of obvious value - and, in addition, we faced a situation where you had both the top hand in a game that was clearly going to end up with one winner and a management with a lot of integrity and intelligence. That one was a real dream. They're very high class people - the Katharine Graham family. That's why it was a dream - an absolute, damn dream. 

Of course, that came about back in '73-'74. And that was almost like 1932. That was probably a once-in-40- years-type denouement in the markets. That investment's up about 50 times over our cost. If I were you, I wouldn't count on getting any investment in your lifetime quite as good as The Washington Post was in '73 and '74.

--from 1994 and/or 1996 transcripts

cost $10M, market $200M...commentary on intrinsic value of $400M to $500M

(1/4)

literally, could not have worked LESS over that time period

call Tim Apple once a year, "u still buying back stock, great, see u next year"

then go back to figuring out "how can I pay 0 taxes on $125B in realized cap gains"

mf ends up owning twelve railroads, the island of Barbados, 17% of Google, and 98% of every bank and conglomerate in Japan

tax bill?

goose egg

shit u do when ur the 🐐

> QT @gregoryblotnick: this message is important, but also nuanced around age (20-40):

"NO ONE CARES HOW HARD YOU WORK."

but u really want to understand this, at all levels, all ages...because like a lot of human nature, its part fact and also part horseshit. 

example one, the idea of "face time." 

1/5

should it matter for ur ass to be in that seat, should it matter that u are "seen" busy and working...no, it shouldnt. ur work should matter.

but DOES it matter?

hell yeah, especially at first

if shit goes wrong, one guy has been "ass in seat," and the other guy has been "working from Jitney" on Fridays...one guy is relatively safe.

or when ppl talk about the first kid, "don't really know him, he works hard tho, he's always here early and stays late"

thats gonna matter. so at first, yes, people do care how hard u work, or more accurately, that u give off the appearance of working.

this is the same "sorta bullshit" bucket as all degrees, certifications, pedigree, CV stamps, et cetera...its 90% bullshit, but it matters.

2/5

NOW...as u move up the layer cake, face time matters less and less, and performance matters more and more. 

at least, it should, or ur career path doesn't have much by way of motivation and upside.

but let's say u turn 30, you've done everything right, and u bounce to go try and build something.

at that moment...you fully enter the "NO ONE CARES HOW HARD YOU WORK" zone.

you have to immediately unlearn and incinerate your 10-20 years of accumulated corporate indoctrination

all that straight up bullshit, politics, face time, etc

on your own, NONE of that shit is relevant.

you either perform or you don't..."losers whine about their best, winners go home and f*** the prom queen," as Sean Connery put it.

3/5

you have partners, investors, employees, an orbit of ppl linked to ur success

for a lot of businesses, that comes down to a quarterly report with a few numbers in it:

"we grew 5%," "we returned 8%," "we lost 1%"

that's it. that number is your job, all these people in your orbit are there for that output

absolutely no one gives a fuck how hard you work.

if u work ur ass off and its a (10%), "maybe dont work so hard next time"

if u spend all month on the beach, do 1 hour of work, and its a +20%... u should do that.

this message, I feel like is not pounded into people's heads enough

especially because u are full of habits from age 20 to 30, that now need to be destroyed as fast as possible, unlearned, ripped out.

investment mgmt can be comical in that your effort and your results, at times, just have zero correlation

we want to believe they do, but the truth is that completely random shit goes down a lot, and it either lifts u or smokes u

4/5

so...in addition to my message of "no one cares how hard u work," there is a LONG journey of self-exploration as u re-optimize ur life

u must keep rebuilding fresh, asking: "how should I spend my days that maximizes my performance?"

this is why I spend so much time talking and thinking about habits and attitude...work matters but the ROI on habits is much much higher

I don't see/hear enough people talking or thinking this way, which is why I'm being forceful on the message.

good habits can bail out bad decisions, but bad habits cant save good decisions.

I might catch a stray for being super results-focused on all this, but u gotta understand, no one ever writes u a check or sends a wire because they think ur a fun guy

Motherfuckers want to get paid

5/5

These are not friendships, don't ever confuse the two...u can test it out firsthand, send a dummy/mistake email that says "we lost 80% yesterday from daytrading crypto" and see how people react

Some people are going to be heated as hell

So take this shit seriously, and question anyone who is like "oh you're too focused on that quarterly performance estimate" or whatever

Out of 100 people, maybe 1 or 2 are dialed in and hyper-competitive, the other 98 are just tossing random comments onto the field. On this topic, find older versions of the 1-2, and listen to them, because they have experience -- the rest has zero.

What do u think people sign an LP agreement for? There IS no other variable, its business. The worst people, the much-maligned types, are dudes who suck ass at their job, but write tons of blogs and letters and do podcasts instead. 

Every LP is thinking "maybe this jackass should do his job instead of blathering about AGI"

Results matter, outcomes matter, proof of concept matters, execution matters, doing exactly what you said you were going to do matters

You do that long enough and maybe u can open ur mouth here and there

But realistically, its in everyones best interest - including your own - to just spend the first 3-5 years doing two things: STFU, and execute.

END

Until u have proven u can execute, there is nothing to talk about.

You worked really long hours last year, but you couldn't execute? 

No one cares. In fact, if u don't execute this year, that's two in a row and most people will (rightfully) redeem, take their ball and go home, and try to find someone who isn't a fucking loser, someone who can execute worth a shit.

Again, if I sound too hard nosed, or this sounds unpleasant, go stay at some giant cushy office job...they are designed around redundancies, mediocrity, and to not miss a beat if u died tomorrow. For some people this sounds great, to others it sounds like prison.

If u want to go do ur own thing, u are the business. So all tolerance for bullshit has to die an immediate death.

I think this principle has been made clear, and ur mind can frame it around 20, 30, 40, or wherever you're at

But the most important message in here, by far, is to recognize when u have entered the "100% results" stage

Because right there, nothing else matters...no one cares how hard you work. Destroy and rebuild ur life from scratch and start the lifelong process of reaching "peak performance" for lack of a better word.

And remember, the stakes are high, but so is the upside...if u come out those first 3-5 years and knock the goddamn cover off the ball, everyone will come in for more, send referrals, and help u with marketing/raising.

The dream has always been there and always will be. There's just a very very small subset of mfs who take risk and bet on themselves to begin with, and then within that subset, 99% will fail.

So if you're that 1%, and you can execute, like, the entire damn world is yours. Straight facts.

Never forget: "Pressure is a privilege." 

So treat it like one...

## 299. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082116490170384590"></a>

*Single post · [2082116490170384590](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082116490170384590)*

“tops are a process, bottoms are an event”

tops, yes
bottoms, not in my experience… “markets are way too cunning.” https://t.co/RjVIUn6tXO

> QT @gregoryblotnick: there's an inverse version of this post - "common investing and market maxims that fail to hold."

two that come to mind (and many more will) are "tops are a process, bottoms are an event" and "buy blood in the streets / be greedy when others are fearful"

its not that any of these maxims are outright wrong -- they survive for a reason -- but they are often misapplied or have nuance that needs to be given

the 2nd one is simpler so we'll start there. what happens is that since it came from Buffett, people take it and then just average down over and over in total piece of shit stocks

"buy blood," "greedy when fearful"

the truth is that until u are an advanced-level investor, or at least have several years of experience, u are prob better off being fearful when others are fearful

or at the very minimum, assume that his advice should be applied strictly to the S&P 500

capitalism is vicious and most businesses fail

I don't want to argue back-and-forth on this one but we can agree that 1. its frequently misapplied, and 2. has blown a shitload of accounts...the idea of being contrarian for contrarian sake, or that "down 90%" = "buy"

--

on "tops as a process, bottoms are an event"

this is semi-true

tops are a process but they are literally impossible to forecast/time...ask anyone who has been trading mkts for a long time and they'll agree, NO ONE with real P&L attached ever nails the top...the whole exercise is bigly negative on an expected value basis

peter lynch's "more $ has been lost trying to prepare for recessions, than in recessions themselves" is the best maxim for tops

tops are sneaky as fuck and quite often, even when its obvious shit is bubbly, things progress another 1-3 years...and a lot of times the biggest gains are at the end

so you're trying to dodge down 20%-30%, and miss the up 50-100%.  plus when ppl call "top," they exit and their mind locks them out, they never get back in...hence from the QT "no one is more bearish than a sold out bull."

a fun exercise (one that LLMs can prob help with) - for any top, pull the major news sources (WSJ/BBG etc) and read the daily headlines from top date plus/minus 4 weeks in each direction...get a feel for what the tone and sentiment was, upload to subconscious, may come in handy someday.

then, on bottoms being an event...this is almost never true.

you can have more luck timing bottoms than u can tops, but go study charts urself, u will see its never what people expect

the "event" resembling, a massive capitulation spike low, huge vomit, price-insensitive selling, gigantic volume, etc

these never turn out to be the bottom -- too obvious -- markets are way too cunning.

from a chart standpoint, the SPX in 2008-2009 is what bottoms usually look like (all of this being my humble and frequently-wrong opinion of course) -- I added the chart below

you had the big vomit in oct 2008, the blood in the streets, the "bottoms are an event"

so people bought, you got the oversold bounce, and then from Oct 2008 to March 2009....the SPX went down another 30% or so

more than anything else, bottoms are marked by APATHY.

every bottom I have ever seen or traded, in a stock or an index, it was always apathy bordering on despair or futility

lost track how many times I had this exact exchange on IB with someone:

me: "are you buying"

them: "no, it only goes down" or "no, I bought the spike low then got stopped out 10% lower, fuck this thing...you?"

me: "exact same thought"

and that very day... was always the bottom. seared into my memory each time

so the context/nuance I'd add, is that all the violence means you're probably CLOSE to a bottom...but not only could the bottom be another 30% away, bottoms are not guaranteed to be V-shaped

especially in individual stocks, sometimes a bottom is years long

the stock is bombed out and not going down any further, it ran out of sellers, but that doesnt mean it magically goes up...something has to change the narrative

and you have to factor in trade duration/opportunity cost of capital

it may be "10% down, 50% up," but having $ tied up earning 0% for years isn't a win when the mkt return is 7-8%

so "value is its own catalyst," sometimes true, sometimes not...the duration of the investment affects whether that's wise or not.

--

there's a lot more of these flawed maxims / ones that are hazardous on a standalone basis

but short term, the top maxims in the QT always seem to be the best go-to, almost Occam's razor-y

the big-brain backfitted answers from media and sales/trading desks always are secondary to stuff like:

"why was GOOG down the most yday," bc everyone's long
"why does the tape suck," bc incrementally hawkish fed

there's never an 100% correct answer but the closest thing to it is, fundamentals are always secondary to "max pain to max # of ppl" and "fed policy/liquidity drives everything."

remember, they still don't know what caused the crash of 1987...so, they prob don't know why the nasdaq was off 150 bps yesterday.

no one knows shit

good luck trading

## 301. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082201511799529767"></a>

*Single post · [2082201511799529767](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082201511799529767)*

if I had to describe the easiest and most straightforward way to make money in markets, its:

1. be in a bull market (usually the case)
2. find stocks that are showing technical and fundamental momentum, breaking to fresh ATH's, positive revisions cycles, and have secular/industry tailwinds if possible
3. buy fucking dips
4. don't fight the fed
5. don't do any fundamental work, it will only confuse u and take ur eye off what matters (riding multi-year winners in size + buying dips)
6. be willing to add size at ATH's if fundamentals transform/accelerate (ie AMZN announces AWS) 
7. stop yourself out when the moving averages start to look like shit (more art than science)

I say all this as a fundamental analyst

Buying and pressing the leading stocks in a bull market is how u double or triple up in 12-24 months

"omg its at 40x EPS" - worthless analysis, esp because the 40x will reveal itself as 20x, and u sold a winner up 40% that went up another 400%

All the other shit that people screw around with is a joke in comparison - if a single dollar is made on NVDA puts this cycle it would be a miracle, or ppl buying VIX, or buying names down 60% w/ the index printing ATH's, these are all "I'd rather be right than make money"

If you're truly a flexible and objective investor u dont care if the AI trade is alive or dead because u see other sectors emerging as new market leadership

Do with this what you will

one addition to this

quite often - maybe even 50% of the time - you will be positioned into an event that makes you say

"Even if I had all the numbers right in front of me, right now, I would still have no fucking clue what the stock is going to do."

this applies for every Fed meeting (for me at least) and prob 50% of earnings prints, as well as every economic data release

the only takeaway I can offer is "this is why you have to extend your time horizon"

because you just see so much T+1 fuckery, which resolves itself eventually...but that could be 3, 6, or 12 months

I used to say "hedge into the event" but the fact that I have no fucking clue, yields a negative trade EV of whatever I blew on premium

if corr is 0, its 0, you are often best saying "I don't know" vs adding complexity that could only fuck things up worse.

TL;DR the market will agree with you if you're right but you never have a goddamn clue when

> QT @gregoryblotnick: The Long Term Is A Series of Short-Terms: Time Arbitrage + Revision Cycles

## 306. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082244505365880851"></a>

*Single post · [2082244505365880851](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082244505365880851)*

BREAKING DOWN TAPE (PART THREE)

the quoted tweet is one that you can, and should, backtest top to bottom

here we will run through all the sort of bullets/checkpoints I laid out, in 10 parts -- no real order

but thru breaking down tape over and over and over for years, and looking at not just ur own trades, but "wtf actually works best in markets," u will develop a list that will resemble mine. 

perhaps not bullet by bullet, but a list of what u believe to work best nonetheless.

so this is separate from grinding ur P&L for losses

this is the omniscient narrator view, watching what others do to win, reconciling it with what you do, and iterating accordingly

--

1

>compile list of tickers; "errors of omission" per Buffett, names u traded poorly, winners u cut too soon

>you can do this for losses also but losses are more straightforward (where was your stop? if u didnt have one, where did u exit? do u have a gameplan for cutting losses or are u just prancing around?)

>for winners that u didnt "win" correctly, so to speak....pull up the chart on tradingview dot com (good scrollable charts that finviz doesnt have)

>go back 5-10 years or whenever inception/entry would have occurred

>and from there...just go forward, scrolling, positive visualization, walking thru what u actually did, vs what you WOULD'VE had to do in order to execute on the trade...entire lifecycle, entry to exit

2

>this is why twtr/paper trading is bullshit and does not replicate the emotional and psychological impact of riding a 20% position over multiple years

>but as you retrace all these winners, u will recognize many of the precursors put in place in the QT...like, if its a bear market or correction tape, it doesnt matter wtf stocks u picked

>the winners, were always printing ATHs, and were in the midst of beat-and-raise cycles that lasted for years on end at times...or, stopped 1 qtr, stock sold off 5-10%, then resumed for 4-8 more qtrs (hence why time horizon matters)

>note what the NTM multiples wouldve optically looked like...they wouldve shaken u out.  so thats why I say ignore fundamentals/valuation, which is NOT the same as "u better know that fucking business cold." because u will never have the conviction for a 20% position if u dont.

3

>I have not had that many monster winners but the ones I did, that were sized like that, I was in my model every single morning...tweaking #'s, seeing if r/r still made sense, seeing if I should buy something else instead

>this is VERY very hard due to "shiny object syndrome." you do not know how hard it is to run with a three-stock, 33% each, Munger-style portfolio, for years on end...until u try it.

>transformational quarter - u often have to buy a stock when its up 20% on a gap, to fresh ATHs. unless u are like a 7/10 level of experience, u are never gonna do this, it takes conviction and brass balls (mostly conviction)

>when to exit the trade, I have no idea, I'm always too early and get shaken out by crappy price action

4

but until u have done this visualization exercise like 100s of times, dont expect that u will ever execute in real time...practice vs gameday...zero reason to expect u will perform on gameday without pattern recognition/playbook

especially in size

all of this with a 1% position is easy

when its 25%, and u see giant P&L dollars flying around, thats when the bitch in you takes over at quarterback

5

as much as that "straightforward/easiest" way can sound dumb or ignorant at times, its mostly pragmatic

again, Buffett's AAPL trade

that was a leading stock during a bull market rally for 5-7 years

u know what he had to do to make money?

nothing

over 7 years, he clicked "buy" two or three times, each with tens of billions of dollars, and then he did nothing

so "be long leading stocks in a bullish tape," why would u complicate it more than that

6

compare and contrast this approach with all the other foolish shit u see people do, fighting a Jihad against some stock that has made new 52w lows every single day for 2 years

like out of this entire tape u decide "ya ORCL is my #1 best idea"

wtf

based on what lmao

like listen to the tape brocephalus

or, some crazy battleground stock that rips 25% every few weeks in a different direction and goes nowhere for 2 years

the applovins or the HIMS or shit like that

people will wrestle with names like these (sized at prob 3%) when there have been technical/fundamental leaders printing fresh ATH's and expanding ROIC for years on end

7

this is when u come full circle as an investor and learn to turn your brain off

why the fuck would I want some complex SOTP turd with 4x leverage and eight segments that has 40% upside if we can figure out what the share count is

on that new highs list there's 5-10 stocks I either know cold (ROST PM KO TGT EA) plus another 10-20 where I can get up to speed in a matter of days

that's where I want to confine all my research efforts

not "wow lululemon looks really interesting down 70% with ROIC down 5000 bps"

8

this fits the same bucket as "no one cares how hard you work"

no one cares if you made a dollar from shorting ORCL CDS and buying the equity, or if you made it from buying Apple as it printed yet another ATH

so the "easy/straightforward" list may sound dumb but its really the blackjack framework

why would I sit at a table where my max odds are 55-45, and then use a strategy where my odds are 42-58

dumb ass shit

9

markets tend to rally and leading stocks tend to have both technical and fundamental momentum, since the chart mirrors equity value creation

guys will see that and say "I'm going to go short NVDA instead" as if that's the highest odds bet on the table

idk how much this message will resonate w/ people, its expeirence-dependent

when I was 5-10 years in, I still wanted to be smart

I wouldnt have been receptive to someone replying to a pitch with "have u thought of just buying Apple"

its only after you've tried everything else that you reduce ur process to what is simple, offers the best odds, and works decade after decade

10

"is this what buffett and munger do?"

honestly, I never thought of that until right now, or at least connected the dots...but yes, there is always that element in everything they say

"too hard pile"
"if its complex, we're not interested"
"lethargy bordering on sloth"

the only difference is that they're more sensitive on price/entry than I am

if I see it on the 52w high list, thats my "pick up the pencil," and I'm probably closer to Druck in just trying to figure out "wtf inning is this trade in?" I'm not trying to play all nine, I'm fine with being there 3rd to the 7th.

...or at least, that's where I am at this very moment.

that list below is the end result of breaking down tape for quite a long time

after you go thru this same process u will have ur own list. and as always, all views are subject to change

"strong opinions, weakly held."

bon courage, mon ami

> QT @gregoryblotnick: if I had to describe the easiest and most straightforward way to make money in markets, its:

2. find stocks that are showing technical and fundamental momentum, breaking to fresh ATH's, positive revisions cycles, and have secular/industry tailwinds if possible
3. buy fucking dips
4. don't fight the fed
5. don't do any fundamental work, it will only confuse u and take ur eye off what matters (riding multi-year winners in size + buying dips)
6. be willing to add size at ATH's if fundamentals transform/accelerate (ie AMZN announces AWS) 
7. stop yourself out when the moving averages start to look like shit (more art than science)

Buying and pressing the leading stocks in a bull market is how u double or triple up in 12-24 months

If you're truly a flexible and objective investor u dont care if the AI trade is alive or dead because u see other sectors emerging as new market leadership

Do with this what you will

### 1/2

*2026-07-28 · [2082250654609703360](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082250654609703360)*

Q: "When did you turn from a loser to a winner?"

quote from one of the O.G. "Market Wizards" - Marty Schwartz...S&P trader, grew a $40k acct to $20M with a max drawdown of 3%

very very common answer amongst guys who have been doing this forever...deceptively simple

A: "When I was able to separate my ego needs from making money. When I was able to accept being wrong. Before, admitting I was wrong was more upsetting than losing the money. I used to try to will things to happen. I figured it out, therefore it can't be wrong. When I became a winner, I said, "I figured it out, but if I'm wrong, I'm getting the hell out, because I want to save my money and go on to the next trade." By living the philosophy that my winners are always in front of me, it is not so painful to take a loss. If I make a mistake, so what!"

### 2/2

*2026-07-28 · [2082250863138021716](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082250863138021716)*

## 308. 2026-07-28
<a id="t-2082252459951419768"></a>

*Single post · [2082252459951419768](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082252459951419768)*

this quote from trader Marty Schwartz sums up a giant cross-section of both trading markets and human nature

Q: Why do most traders lose money?

A: Because they would rather lose money than admit they're wrong. What is the ultimate rationalization of a trader in a losing position? "I'll get out when I'm even." Why is getting out even so important? Because it protects the ego. I became a winning trader when I was able to say, "To hell with my ego, making money is more important."

--

very very common answer amongst guys who have been doing this forever...deceptively simple

A: "When I was able to separate my ego needs from making money. When I was able to accept being wrong. Before, admitting I was wrong was more upsetting than losing the money. I used to try to will things to happen. I figured it out, therefore it can't be wrong. When I became a winner, I said, "I figured it out, but if I'm wrong, I'm getting the hell out, because I want to save my money and go on to the next trade." By living the philosophy that my winners are always in front of me, it is not so painful to take a loss. If I make a mistake, so what!"

not sure if Tom Russo's style goes out of favor but the simple checklist of "capacity to suffer" + capacity to reinvest + family ownership...very hard for those to get too fucked up, tend to o/p on relative basis

"The three prongs that I look for when investing in a business are: the fifty cent dollar bill, the capacity to reinvest in great brands and the “capacity to suffer.” 

"The “capacity to suffer” is key because often the initial spending to build on these great brands in new markets has no initial return. Many companies will try to invest smoothly over time with no burden on currently reported net income, but the problem is that when you are trying to invest in a new market, smooth investment spending really doesn’t give you enough power to make an impression. You end up letting in a lot of competition that will drive down future margins."

events where u could give me the numbers in advance and I'd still get the move wrong:

-hyperscaler capex guides (even up/down)
-nvda/mu/sndk guides
-anything involving Warsh
-CPI/ISM
-effect of risk-free rate (10yr/30yr) on SPX multiple

> QT @gregoryblotnick: one addition to this

quite often - maybe even 50% of the time - you will be positioned into an event that makes you say

"Even if I had all the numbers right in front of me, right now, I would still have no fucking clue what the stock is going to do."

this applies for every Fed meeting (for me at least) and prob 50% of earnings prints, as well as every economic data release

the only takeaway I can offer is "this is why you have to extend your time horizon"

because you just see so much T+1 fuckery, which resolves itself eventually...but that could be 3, 6, or 12 months

I used to say "hedge into the event" but the fact that I have no fucking clue, yields a negative trade EV of whatever I blew on premium

if corr is 0, its 0, you are often best saying "I don't know" vs adding complexity that could only fuck things up worse.

TL;DR the market will agree with you if you're right but you never have a goddamn clue when

## 314. 2026-07-29
<a id="t-2082538298619371693"></a>

*Single post · [2082538298619371693](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082538298619371693)*

"BEARISH AND LONG" -- gotten tons of mileage out of this janky little homemade meme

actually sums up an enormous amount of market intelligence + mental acuity + innate cognitive biases, all in a single image

"bearish and long" goes against something hardwired in our mind...u gotta keep pushing back at it, hacking away, removing that embedded emotional response. 

two direct quotes below

--

"The degree to which you think you know, assume you know, or in any way need to know what is going to happen next is equal to the degree to which you will fail as a trader." Mark Douglas

--

These are the three stages of the game.

1. Beginner: Always bullish, always long.

2. Intermediate: You get smarter, you start getting bearish and shorting shit.

3. Advanced: At the highest level, you reach the ability to be "BEARISH AND LONG."

Many never reach this third stage. It requires a pretty powerful mind, the ability to hold two opposing ideas in the mind simultaneously.

F. Scott Fitzgerald: "The test of a first-rate intelligence is the ability to hold two opposed ideas in the mind at the same time, and still retain the ability to function."

> QT @gregoryblotnick: Reading The Tape (Part 2) - "BEARISH AND LONG," Event Path & Cognitive Bias

## 317. 2026-07-30
<a id="t-2082793086401364082"></a>

*Single post · [2082793086401364082](https://x.com/gregoryblotnick/status/2082793086401364082)*

re: fundamental/bonds/macro rabbit hole avoidance

there exists some connection between A. 30-yr treasury yields breaking out to 19-year highs B. poor price action in long duration/terminal value-centric equities…aka, AI stonks and futuretech

from experience u don’t want to go too deep into this, bc u quickly encounter false precision, confirmation bias, and hordes of ppl ascribing too much credit to one side of the story

“fundamentals are fine, it’s all rates” is wrong (bullish conf bias)

“fundamentals are fucked up, it’s not about rates” is also wrong (bearish conf bias)

the truth is somewhere in the middle

but if u are a fundamental analyst u are prob best off in “more art than science” territory here…the mega-precise attributions for px action can be both dangerously seductive and dead ass wrong

“who the fuck knows” isn’t an answer that gets u paid subs, it makes u sound flaccid rather than intelligent… but, its often the best possible answer

the 30 yr yield can change for a hundred different reasons, all w different implications, and without a bbg terminal u don’t even have the inputs to try and backsolve all this shit

last, what would buffett say, prob “when u own shit for 3-5+ years, u don’t give a fuck about what treasury yields did on any given day.”

for further questions on this I’d go to LLM’s - see QT for examples of ways u could chop this inquiry up, angles of attack, etc

> QT @gregoryblotnick: the best feedback I got on this, from ppl I look up to and respect:

"more focus on bond market/fixed income"

totally agree. I was trying to straddle the line on "equity analyst focus" but if there's one area to go overboard, its understanding bonds/cap structure

will just straight up copy+paste other ppls feedback:

--

1. "the bond mkt is a valuable source of information.. especially if you believe in the wisdom of crowds.. & in case of the bond mkt. the crowd is massive, has lots of capital to deploy & tends to focus on cashflows rather than extrapolating the past"

2. "there are bonds in the stocks - (one can think of what Mauboussin calls the mkt implied forecast period as a form of equity duration)"

3. "monitoring 1 month 25 delta put-call skew & iv rank / percentiles across indicies rates & commodities can be helpful... but what adds value is being able to decompose the move. eg. knowing that if rates move because growth expectations improve equities tend to digest that fine... if a move is due to a "pure" rise in discount rates, then long duration momo and other factors can get absolutely clubbed... (fundamentals can go out of the window if a few big holders decide to have a little trimm.. & all the algos pile on etc.)... knowing what the moves mean and when/why they could matter for equities (& more specifically which factors)... not to trade SOFR or what have you..."

--

the most important phrase, in there is understanding the concept of "equity duration" in the context of fixed income duration

or how buffett tends to gravitate towards "equity bonds" as investments -- i.e. a consumer staples stock like KO with a bond-ish cash flow stream/return profile

its important as an equity investor to understand that you are the most junior tranche, and the 1000 sort of paths to get there and implications...I think in the article I mentioned "understand how distressed debt behaves like equity," u can hit LLMs with that all day, develop a seamless understanding of debt as debt, equity as equity, and the sliding scale up/down a cap structure how those change...when a bond stops trading on yield/YTM is a meaningless #, and represents equity/a call option on recovery...I can't pinpoint "here's how this will help you," but it will.

and feedback was consistent on "good work telling ppl to stay the hell out of all the global macro/fx/etc" -- rabbit hole with extremely poor return on time for an equity analyst.

for now...classic 1977 buffett article on inflation re: equity investors...good a place as any to start

https://t.co/35dYd0sblG
